, a small neighborhood of
\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}}
in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
was explicitly constructed as a
single
regular curve emanating from each special pair. This will in fact imply the transvesrality of
\overline{\mathcal{R}^{L}},\overline{\mathcal{R}^{U}}
with respect to
\Delta_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 177
.

Let
K
be generic. If
x\in\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}^{-1}(0)
, then
\hbox{rank}(d{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}(x))=6
.

Moreover, let
v\in T_{\pi_{s_{1},s_{1}}(x)}(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
. Then
v\pitchfork\mathcal{R}
, iff the vectors

\{({\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}})_{\ast}v,\dot{\gamma}(y_{3}),\ell\gamma(s_{2})-\ell\gamma(s_{1})\}

are linearly independent.

Proof.

The lemma can be seen as a corollary of the proof of Lemma
175
(i.e.
[
19
, lem 7.10]
), where one had used twice Thom’s Transversality Theorem
33
.

In more detail. The condition “four vectors from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
lie in a plane” is of codim
2
. Hence, from the matrix
d{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}(x)
(Remark
174
), we see that the condition “
\hbox{rank}(d{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}(x))<6
, where
x\in\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}^{-1}(0)
” is of codim
3+3+2=8
. Thus, it is avoidable for generic knots.

In the second statement, one obtains analogously that for a generic knot the vectors
\dot{\gamma}(y_{3}),\ell\gamma(s_{2})-\ell\gamma(s_{1})
are linearly independent. Indeed, the condition “two vectors from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
are linearly dependent” is of codim
2
. Finally, since
T_{x}({\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}^{-1}(0))=\ker(d{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}(x))
,
({\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}})_{\ast}v
could not lie in the
span
of
\dot{\gamma}(y_{3}),\ell\gamma(s_{2})-\ell\gamma(s_{1})
. So the second statement follows.
∎

Lemma 178
.

For a generic knot
K
it holds that

Crit(E_{0})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}}=Crit(E_{0})\cap\mathcal{R}^{L}=Crit(E_{0})\cap\mathcal{R}^{U}=\emptyset.

Proof.

By Lemma
96
we know that for a generic
K
the set
Crit(E_{0})\setminus\Delta_{0}
consists of a finite number of nondegenerate critical points. In particular,
Crit(E_{0})\setminus\Delta_{0}
is stable under perturbations of
K
(in the sense of Stability Lemma
31
for the perturbations of the map
(\gamma\times\gamma)^{\ast}E
). Hence, for a generic knot, we can assume that any two binormal chords are not parallel. In particular, if
x\in\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}^{-1}(0)
, then at most one point of
\pi_{s_{1},s_{2}}(x),\pi_{y_{1},y_{3}}(x),\pi_{z_{2},z_{3}}(x)
lies in
Crit(E_{0})
. Then the lemma follows from perturbations of
K
as in
[
90
, lem 2.17]
.
∎

Lemma 179
.

Let
K
be a generic knot and
X_{E_{0}}
be a gradient-like vector field. Then there is a gradient-like vector field
Y_{E_{0}}
such that
Y_{E_{0}}
is tangent to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
at only a finite number of the points and
X_{E_{0}}
and
Y_{E_{0}}
are
C^{1}
close.

Proof.

By Lemma
175
we know that
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is a compact immersed
1
-manifold with boundary and a finite number of transverse self-intersections of the covering degree
2
. Hence there is a finite covering
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,k}
of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
by compact connected
1
-manifolds
R_{i}
embedded in
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
. Moreover, there are open subsets
U_{i}\subset(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
such that
R_{i}\subset U_{i}
and
\partial\overline{U_{i}}\pitchfork\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Let us put
Y_{0}:=X_{E_{0}}
. By an induction in
i
we would like to construct perturbed vector fields
Y_{i}
such that
Y_{i}\pitchfork\overline{\mathcal{R}}
along
R_{i}
and
Y_{i}=Y_{i-1}
on
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\setminus\overline{U_{i}}
(see below). Then we put
Y_{E_{0}}:=Y_{k}
. If for each
i
the perturbation is sufficiently small in
C^{\infty}
topology (relative to the perturbations at the steps
1,\dots,i-1
), then by Stability Lemma
31
the vector field
Y_{E_{0}}
has the desired tangency properties and is
C^{1}
close to
X_{E_{0}}
. Moreover, by Lemma
178

Y_{E_{0}}
is gradient-like.

Let us discuss the
i
-th perturbation. For simplicity of the notation, we assume that
R_{i}
does not contain any self-intersection of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Let
R^{ext}_{i}
be a small open extension of
R_{i}
inside
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(if
R_{i}\cap\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}}\neq\emptyset
, then we consider, instead of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, any small auxiliary extension of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
beyond the boundary
\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}}
). Now, we can assume that a small tubular neighborhood
\nu(R^{ext}_{i})
induces a chart
\varphi:\nu(R^{ext}_{i})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{x,y}
such that

\overline{U_{i}}=[-2,2]\times[-1,1],\quad R^{ext}_{i}\cap\overline{U_{i}}=[-2,2]\times\{0\},\quad R_{i}=[-1,1]\times\{0\}.

Let
\widetilde{Y}_{i-1}:=\varphi_{\ast}Y_{i-1}\varphi^{-1}
. Then by Relative Thom’s transversality Theorem
[
90
, thm A.4]
there is a
C^{1}
small function
\rho:[-2,2]\times\{0\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
dy(\widetilde{Y}_{i-1})+\rho\pitchfork 0
along
[-2,2]\times\{0\}
and
\rho(\{\pm 2\}\times\{0\})=0.
Now we can extend
\rho
to the whole
\overline{U}_{i}
as
\sigma(y)\rho(x)
, where
\sigma(y)
is a small bump function which is supported on
[-1,1]
. Then
\sigma\rho\partial_{y}
determines the desired perturbation of
Y_{i-1}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
170
. Lemma
175
and Corrollary
176
give us a recipe, how to use the
forward flow

\phi^{t}_{X_{E_{0}}}
and iteratively construct in the configuration space
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
all trees of
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{0}}}
.

That is the following. We consider
p_{0}\in Crit_{1}(E_{0})\setminus\Delta_{0}
and
x\in W^{u}_{X_{E_{0}}}(p_{0})
that is close to
p_{0}
. Then we continue flowing with
\phi^{t}_{X_{E_{0}}}(x)
until we hit
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, ideally in
\mathcal{R}\setminus\lozenge\mathcal{R}
. Then we can bifurcate and jump with the maps
\varphi^{L/U}
. After that, we continue by flowing and repeat.

Hence, what we need to achieve is that each potential intersection of the (partial) flow trajectory with
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is transverse and avoid
\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}},\lozenge\mathcal{R}
. Moreover, we need to avoid
Crit(E_{0})
besides the root and the leaves. About
\Delta_{0}
and
\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, we do not need to worry. Indeed, we are not counting trees with leaves in
\Delta_{0}
, and
\Delta_{0}
is the global minimum of
E_{0}
. So, we can take a small regular index pair
(N,\emptyset)
for
\Delta_{0}
and study the flow only on
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\setminus Int(N).
In particular, any bifurcation will shrink the chords at least by some positive constant. Since the gradient-like vector field flows downhill, the number of potential bifurcations is bounded from above.

By Lemma
179
, we can assume that there is only a
finite
number of problematic points along the whole
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Now, a similar
finite
inductive argument as in Corollary
121
will result in a desired perturbed gradient-like vector field. We remark that the local perturbations around the intersections with
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
are described in
[
90
, lem 2.20]
, see also Lemma
120
.

∎

Remark 180
.

Since by Lemma
175

\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}}
corresponds to the special points, we achieved in Lemma
170
that the trees
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{0}}}
(when realized in
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
) all lie in a standard set
S_{K}
. Here we implicitly assumed that the constant
\delta_{K}>0
for
S_{K}
is sufficiently small, see Remark
47
.

Set-up 181
.

Let us fix
m\in\mathbb{N},\mathcal{T}\in\clubsuit_{m},p_{0}\in Crit_{1}(E_{0})\setminus\Delta_{0}
and
q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}\in Crit_{0}(E_{0})\setminus\Delta_{0}
and a gradient-like vector field
X_{E_{0}}
.

The following exposition is motivated by
[
72
]
, see also
[
2
,
70
]
.

Remark 182
.

Recall that
m=|V_{leaf}(\mathcal{T})|=|V_{int}(\mathcal{T})|+1=|E_{int}(\mathcal{T})|+2
.

Definition 183
.

We define a map

\displaystyle i_{time}:(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}

\displaystyle(s_{1},s_{2},t)\hskip 22.76228pt

\displaystyle\longmapsto\hskip 2.84544pt(s_{1},s_{2},\phi^{t}_{X_{E_{0}}}(s_{1},s_{2})).

(Here
\mathbb{R}_{+}=(0,\infty)
.) The domain
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
will be called as the
space of positive finite length trajectories
.

Remark 184
.

Since
\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=t_{0}}\phi^{t}_{X_{E_{0}}}(s_{1},s_{2})=X_{E_{0}}(\phi^{t_{0}}_{X_{E_{0}}}(s_{1},s_{2}))
, the map
i_{time}
has full rank, when restricted outside of
Crit(E_{0})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In fact, because
X_{E_{0}}
is a gradient-like vector field, the map
i_{time}
is an embedding when restricted outside of
Crit(E_{0})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Definition 185
.

The map

\displaystyle i_{can}:\quad

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}[0,1]\times W^{u}_{X_{E_{0}}}(p_{0})\times\prod_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\big]\times\prod^{m}_{i=1}W^{s}_{X_{E_{0}}}(q_{0}^{i})\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}[0,1]\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\prod_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\big]\times\prod^{m}_{i=1}(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}

is induced by
i_{time}
and canonical inclusions on the remaining terms. (We assume that
E_{int}(\mathcal{T})
and
V_{int}(\mathcal{T})
are arbitrarily ordered.)

Definition 186
.

The
reordering map

\rho_{\mathcal{T}}:

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}[0,1]\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\prod_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})}(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{4}\times\prod^{m}_{i=1}(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[[0,1]\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{6}\big],

\displaystyle((\ell_{v})_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})},a^{0},(a_{\emph{e}}^{in},a_{\emph{e}}^{out})_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})},a^{1},\dots,a^{m})\hskip 22.76228pt

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt(\ell_{v},b_{v},b_{v}^{L},b_{v}^{U})_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}

will be defined by permuting the coordinates as follows. Here letters “
a
” or “
b
” represent, in fact, pairs of coordinates.

To each pair of
a
-coordinates or
b
-coordinates, we would like to assign a flag. If two pairs of
a
-coordinates and
b
-coordinates will have assigned the same flag, then they will be canonically identified under
\rho_{\mathcal{T}}
. For this, we will use the notions from Remarks
165
and
166
.

•

To each of
a^{0},a^{1},\dots,a^{m}
we assign the unique edge
\emph{e}
such that the root or the
i
-th leaf belongs to
\emph{e}

(i\in\{1,\dots,m\})
. The assigned vertex will be the unique interior vertex that belongs to
\emph{e}
.

•

To each
a^{in/out}_{\emph{e}}
we assign the edge
\emph{e}
and the vertex
v^{in/out}_{\emph{e}}
.

•

To each of
b_{v},b_{v}^{L},b_{v}^{U}
we assign the vertex
v
and the edges
\emph{e}_{v},\emph{e}_{v}^{L},\emph{e}_{v}^{U}
, respectively.

Definition 187
.

Let
X_{E_{0}}
be a gradient-like vector field. Then we define the evaluation map
\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}}:

\hskip-25.6073pt\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}[0,1]\times W^{u}_{X_{E_{0}}}(p_{0})\times\prod_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\big]\times\prod^{m}_{i=1}W^{s}_{X_{E_{0}}}(q_{0}^{i})\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}]

as

\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}}:=\big(\prod_{v\in V_{int}}{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}\big)\circ\rho_{\mathcal{T}}\circ i_{can}.

Corollary 188
.

For
K
generic, there is a canonical bijection

\clubsuit_{X_{E_{0}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}){\mathrel{\to}^{1-1}}(\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}})^{-1}(0).

In particular, if
X_{E_{0}}
is a generic gradient-like vector field from Lemma
170
(which was constructed by certain perturbations in the configuration space
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
), then

\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}}\pitchfork 0.

(5.1)

Proof.

The bijection follows from the construction of
\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}}
. Here we remark that
\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}}
does not count trees with edges of constant paths. Indeed, for interior edges it is clear, and for the remaining edges it follows from
\mathcal{R}\cap Crit(E_{0})=\emptyset
. Also for every
v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})
it holds that
(\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{0};q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}})^{-1}(0)\cap\pi_{\ell_{v}}^{-1}(\{0,1\})=\emptyset
. This follows from the fact that
p_{0},q_{0}^{1},\dots,q_{0}^{m}\in Crit(E_{0})\setminus\Delta_{0}
, see also Remark
180
.

The transversality (
5.1
) then follows from Lemma
177
, see also Remark
174
for the description of
d{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}
.
∎

We finish the section with a small discussion about the evaluation map
{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}
.

Remark 189
.

It is easy to see that
\pi_{s_{1},s_{2}}({\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}^{-1}(0))=(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2},
which is too big space. We have also introduced the restriction
\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}
, where
\pi_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}^{-1}(0))=\mathcal{R}
. By Lemma
175

\mathcal{R}
has a nice geometric picture, and moreover allows us to describe all bifurcations of
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{0}}}
. However, for further work, it will be a bit inconvenient that the domain of
\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}
, and in particular also
\mathcal{R}
, are both non-compact. Luckily, we have seen in Remark
180
that in fact all bifurcations of
\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}
have to appear outside of a small neighborhood of
\partial\overline{\mathcal{R}}.
So it will be enough to consider only certain restrictions of
\mathcal{R}
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 190
.

\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}}
will denote the restriction of
{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}
to
[0,1]\times((\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\setminus\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0}))^{3}
, where
\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0})
is a
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}
-radius (open) tubular neighborhood of
\Delta_{0}
for some
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}>0
small.

Then the canonical projections
\pi_{s_{1},s_{2}},\pi_{y_{1},y_{3}},\pi_{z_{2},z_{3}}
on
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}}}^{-1}(0)
will define the sets
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}},\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}^{L}}},\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}^{U}}},
respectively.

Remark 191
.

If
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}>0
is small and
K
generic, then by Lemma
175
and Lemma
176
the following holds.
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}}
is a compact immersed
1
-dimensional submanifold of
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
with boundary. Moreover, outside of small neighborhoods of special points
\mathcal{R}
and
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}}
coincide and
\widetilde{d}_{Haus}(\mathcal{R},\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}})\rightarrow 0
as
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow 0
.

5.2
Trees for tori

We would like to define Morse flow trees on
T_{K,\varepsilon}\times T_{K,\varepsilon}
(on
M_{K,\varepsilon}
) in a similar flavor as was done in the knot case.

Remark/Notation 192
.

Similarly to the knot case, we will be using three notions for
chords on
T_{K,\varepsilon}
. If
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}:(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the parametrization of
T_{K,\varepsilon}
from Remark
38
, then any chord can be seen as a quadruple
(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2})\in(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}
or a as vector
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(s_{2},\theta_{2})-\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(s_{1},\theta_{1})
. Moreover, the chord can also be seen as a smooth map
c_{s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}}^{\varepsilon}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by

c_{s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}}^{\varepsilon}(\ell):=(1-\ell)\gamma(s_{1})+\ell\gamma(s_{2})+\varepsilon\big[(1-\ell)v(s_{1},\theta_{1})+\ell v(s_{2},\theta_{2})\big].

Here recall that in Remark
38
we introduced
v(s_{i},\theta_{i})=\cos(\theta_{i})n(s_{i})+\sin(\theta_{i})b(s_{i})
.

Definition 193
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{good}]
. Let
p_{\varepsilon}\in Crit_{2}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
and
q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in Crit_{1}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
such that
p_{\varepsilon},q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in M_{K,\varepsilon}
. Let
\mathcal{T}\in\clubsuit_{m}.
Let
X_{E_{\varepsilon}}
be a gradient-like vector field adapted to
E_{\varepsilon}
. A
Morse flow tree from
p_{\varepsilon}
to
q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}
modeled on
\mathcal{T}
(in the configuration space
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}
)
is an ordered tree
\mathcal{T}
together with the following assignment:

(i.)

If
m=1
then the unique edge corresponds to a standard flow line
\phi_{X_{E_{0}}}^{t}
with
t\in\mathbb{R}.

If
m>1
, then the interior edges correspond to
positive finite length
partial flow lines of
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{t}
. The other edges correspond to partial flow lines of
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{t}
parametrized by
t\in[0,\infty)
or
t\in(-\infty,0]
.

(ii.)

The root corresponds to
p_{\varepsilon}
.

(iii.)

i
-th leaf is identified with
q_{\varepsilon}^{i}
.

(iv.)

The tree is allowed to bifurcate at the points where the flow lines consist of nontrivial chords intersecting
T_{K,\varepsilon}
by their interior.

Let
(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2})
be such a chord and
(s_{3},\theta_{2})
corresponds to the intersection of
c_{s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}}^{\varepsilon}|_{(0,1)}
with
T_{K,\varepsilon}
. Then, we jump to the chords
(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{3},\theta_{3})
and
(s_{3},\theta_{3},s_{2},\theta_{2})
and continue to flow with
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{t}
. Now, the triple

\{(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}),(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{3},\theta_{3}),(s_{3},\theta_{3},s_{2},\theta_{2})\}

is assigned to the corresponding interior vertex
v
of the tree
\mathcal{T}
. The partial flow-line starting from
(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{3},\theta_{3})
corresponds to the edge
\emph{e}^{L}_{v}
and the partial flow-line starting from
(s_{3},\theta_{3},s_{2},\theta_{2})
corresponds to the edge
\emph{e}^{U}_{v}
.

The
set of all Morse flow trees from
p_{\varepsilon}
to
q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}
modeled on
\mathcal{T}
is denoted by

\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}).

The set of all Morse flow trees for all choices of
m,\mathcal{T},p_{\varepsilon}\in Crit_{2}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
and
q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in Crit_{1}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
will be denoted by
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
.

If
m=1
, we mod out the natural
\mathbb{R}
-action, then

\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1})=\mathcal{M}_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1}).

Remark 194
.

If in Definition
193
we demand that at least one edge corresponds to a constant or broken (partial) flow line of
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
, then the resulted tree will be called
broken Morse flow tree
.

Again, as in Remark
169
, we stress out that even for the broken Morse flow trees we are not allowing that
q_{\varepsilon}^{i}\in\Delta_{full}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}.

Definition 195
.

We put

\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{out-out}:=\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}\cap M_{K,\varepsilon}.

I. e.
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{out-out}
consists of Morse flow trees of
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
that lie completely in
M_{K,\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 196
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{good}]
. Let
p_{\varepsilon}\in Crit_{2}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
and
q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in Crit_{1}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
such that
p_{\varepsilon},q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in M_{K,\varepsilon}
. If
K
is a generic knot,
\varepsilon>0
is small,
X_{E_{\varepsilon}}
is a generic perturbation of
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
, then
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m})
is a zero dimensional compact manifold. In addition, there are no broken Morse flow trees,
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}=\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{out-out}
and for
m\gg 0
the set
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m})
is empty.

Remark 197
.

Now, we are going to show a heuristic proof of the dimension count for
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m})
. The precise proof of Lemma
170
will be the aim of Chapter
6
, where we will compare
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
with
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{0}}}
using the Multiple time scale dynamics together with the compactness argument motivated by Chapter
4
.

Heuristic proof.

For
m=1
, the argument is the same as in Lemma
170
. For
m>1
we obtain
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m})
as an intersection of certain transversality conditions; let us compute

\displaystyle\dim\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}

\displaystyle Ind_{E_{\varepsilon}}(p_{\varepsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(4-Ind_{E_{\varepsilon}}(q_{\varepsilon}^{i}))+\sum_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})}(4+1)-\sum_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}(2\cdot 4\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+\,0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Ind_{E_{\varepsilon}}(p_{\varepsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(4-Ind_{E_{\varepsilon}}(q_{\varepsilon}^{i}))+(m-2)(4+1)-(m-1)(2\cdot 4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Ind_{E_{\varepsilon}}(p_{\varepsilon})-\sum_{i=1}^{m}Ind_{E_{\varepsilon}}(q_{\varepsilon}^{i})+m-2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

The step
(1.)
is similar to that in Lemma
170
except that now, the constraint for a chord to intersect
T_{K,\varepsilon}
with its interior is of
\codim 0
.
∎

Definition 198
.

Let
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{good}]
(i.e.
\varepsilon
can be even
0
). Let us introduce the evaluation map

{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}:[0,1]\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}\rightarrow(S^{1})^{3}\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

as

\begin{pmatrix}\ell\\
s_{1}\\
\theta_{1}\\
s_{2}\\
\theta_{2}\\
y_{1}\\
\alpha_{1}\\
y_{3}\\
\alpha_{3}\\
z_{2}\\
\beta_{2}\\
z_{3}\\
\beta_{3}\\
\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}-\alpha_{1}\\
\theta_{2}-\beta_{2}\\
\alpha_{3}-\beta_{3}\\
s_{1}-y_{1}\\
s_{2}-z_{2}\\
y_{3}-z_{3}\\
\gamma(y_{3})-(1-\ell)\gamma(s_{1})-\ell\gamma(s_{2})+\varepsilon\big[v(y_{3},\alpha_{3})-(1-\ell)v(s_{1},\theta_{1})-\ell v(s_{2},\theta_{2})\big]\end{pmatrix}

where the coordinates
((s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}),(y_{1},\alpha_{1},y_{3},\alpha_{3}),(z_{2},\beta_{2},z_{3},\beta_{3}))
are induced by a single parametrization
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1}\rightarrow T_{K,\varepsilon}
. Also, by Remark
43

{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}
is a well-defined smooth map.

Next,
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}
will denote the restriction of
{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}
to

[0,1]\times\big((\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}\setminus(\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0})\times(S^{1})^{2})\big)^{3}

for some
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}>0
small. Then the subset
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}}}\subset(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}
is defined as

\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}}}=\pi_{s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}}\big(\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)\big).

We also define two sets

\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{L}}}:=\pi_{y_{1},y_{3}}\big(\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)\big)\hbox{ and }\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{U}}}:=\pi_{z_{2},z_{3}}\big(\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)\big).

See also Figure
5.4
.

\labellist
\pinlabel

c_{s_{1},s_{2}}
at 57 715

\pinlabel
c_{y_{1},y_{3}}
at 127 670

\endlabellist

Figure 5.4
:
On the left: for
\varepsilon>0
we can see geometrically a zero of
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}
as a coincidence of
c^{\varepsilon}_{s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}}
with the union of
c^{\varepsilon}_{y_{1},\alpha_{1},y_{3},\alpha_{3}}
and
c^{\varepsilon}_{z_{3},\beta_{3},z_{2},\beta_{2}}
. Two possible coincidences are depicted as the dashed chords with three marked points. Note that we are not imposing any outward-pointing condition.
On the right: an example of a not allowed coincidence, since we are not counting intersections that are close in knot coordinates.

Remark 199
.

Note that the family
\{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{good}]}
is smooth. Moreover

{\rm ev}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}-\alpha_{1}\\
\theta_{2}-\beta_{2}\\
\alpha_{3}-\beta_{3}\\
{\rm ev}_{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(5.2)

and

\mathcal{R}_{0}\cong\mathcal{R}\times(S^{1})^{2}.

Lemma 200
.

For a generic knot and
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}>0
sufficiently small, there is a
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\{\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)\}_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
is a smooth family of isotopic
4
-dimensional compact submanifolds with boundary.

Proof.

Our aim is to apply the Stability Lemma
31
. Hence, we need to verify that for a generic knot the map
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{0}}}
is stratum transverse to
0
.

If we restrict
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{0}}}
to
Int(Dom(\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{0}}}))
, then the transversality is straightforward by relation
(\ref{eqn_ev0})
and Lemma
177
. By Lemmata
175
and
176
, provided that
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, it holds

•

\overline{\mathcal{R}^{L}},\overline{\mathcal{R}^{U}}\pitchfork\overline{\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0})}.

•

\{x\in\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)\,|\,\pi_{y_{1},y_{3}}(x)\in\overline{\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0})}\wedge\pi_{z_{2},z_{3}}(x)\in\overline{\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0})}\}=\emptyset
.

•

\overline{\mathcal{R}}\cap\overline{\nu_{\delta_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Delta_{0})}=\emptyset.

In particular,
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{0}}}^{-1}(0)\cap\pi_{\ell}^{-1}(\{0,1\})=\emptyset
. Which implies the transversality for the remaining cases.
∎

Lemma 201
.

For a generic knot and
\delta_{\mathcal{R}},\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small it holds that

Crit(E_{\varepsilon})\cap\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}}}=Crit(E_{\varepsilon})\cap\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{L}}}=Crit(E_{\varepsilon})\cap\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{U}}}=\emptyset.

Proof.

By Lemma
178
we know that for generic
K
it holds that
Crit(E_{0})\cap\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}}=\emptyset
. Hence by Lemma
99
(the relations
(\ref{eqn_of_crit_pt})
) we obtain that
Crit(E_{\varepsilon})\cap\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{0}}}=\emptyset
. Since
E_{\varepsilon}
is Morse-Bott, the critical submanifolds are isolated. So the isotopy from Lemma
200
finishes the proof, provided that
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small. Other cases are analogous.
∎

Lemma 202
.

For a generic knot and
\delta_{\mathcal{R}},\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small it holds that each bifurcation of
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
appears at
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}}}
.

Proof.

Note that there is a
\delta_{K}>0
small such that for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small

\big(\{(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2})\in(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}\,|\,\widetilde{d}(s_{1},s_{2})\leq\delta_{K}\},\emptyset\big)

is a regular index pair of
\Delta_{full}
under the flow
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
. Also, similarly to the knot case, bifurcations are shortening the chords, so the forward-flow
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
flows along
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
downhill. Hence, since elements of
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}
have no leaves in
\Delta_{full}
, for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small
\pi_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}})
avoids a
\delta_{K}
-neighborhood of any special point. Now, we take
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}>0
such that
\delta_{\mathcal{R}}\ll\delta_{K}
which determines
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}}}
with the desired property.
∎

Definition 203
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{good}]
. Let
p_{\varepsilon}\in Crit_{2}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
and
q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in Crit_{1}(E_{\varepsilon}|_{M_{K,\varepsilon}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}})
such that
p_{\varepsilon},q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}\in M_{K,\varepsilon}
. Let
\mathcal{T}\in\clubsuit_{m}.
Let
X_{E_{\varepsilon}}
be a gradient-like vector field adapted to
E_{\varepsilon}
.

Then we define the evaluation map
\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}}^{\varepsilon}:

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}[0,1]\times W^{u}_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(p_{\varepsilon})\times\prod_{\emph{e}\in E_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\big]\times\prod^{m}_{i=1}W^{s}_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(q_{\varepsilon}^{i})\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}\big[(S^{1})^{3}\times(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}]

as

\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}}^{\varepsilon}=\big(\prod_{v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})}{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}\big)\circ\rho_{\mathcal{T}}\circ i_{can}

where the definitions of the reordering function
\rho_{\mathcal{T}}
and
i_{can}
are analogous to Definitions
185
and
186
.

Corollary 204
.

For
K
generic,
\delta_{\mathcal{R}},\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small there is a canonical bijection

\clubsuit_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}){\mathrel{\to}^{1-1}}(\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}})^{-1}(0).

Proof.

Similarly as in Corollary
188
, the bijection follows from the construction of
\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}}
. Here we remark that
\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}}
also does not count trees with edges of constant paths. Indeed, for interior edges it is clear, and for the remaining edges it follows from Lemmata
201
and
202
. Also by Lemma
202
, for every
v\in V_{int}(\mathcal{T})
it holds that
(\emph{Ev}_{\mathcal{T};p_{\varepsilon};q_{\varepsilon}^{1},\dots,q_{\varepsilon}^{m}})^{-1}(0)\cap\pi_{\ell_{v}}^{-1}(\{0,1\})=\emptyset
.
∎

Remark 205
.

Recall that in the knot case we also used an iterative construction of
\clubsuit_{X_{E_{0}}}
under the
forward-flow

\phi_{X_{E_{0}}}^{t}
. I. e., roughly speaking, we track
W^{u}_{X_{E_{0}}}(p_{0})
for some
p_{0}\in Crit_{1}(E_{0})
. When we hit
\mathcal{R}
, we bifurcate and continue flowing along the new branches and repeat.

However, for the torus case, this method is not very convenient. If we start naively to flow along some trajectory from
p_{\varepsilon}\in Crit_{2}(E_{\varepsilon})
, then we realize that suddenly after the first bifurcation we have to follow two
1
-dimensional families of trajectories and so on. Hence, it might look that the dimensions of the tracked trajectories are exploding after each bifurcation. Fortunately, this all will get corrected once we reach the leaves. Contrary to the knot case, now the constraints to reach the stable manifolds at leaves are of
\codim 1
, which will reduce the dimension of the tracked trajectories.

In order to avoid these temporary explosions of the dimension of tracked trajectories, we will be considering in the torus case an iterative construction under the
backward-flow

\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{-t}
. I. e., roughly speaking, we start to track two
3
-dimensional stable manifolds of two leaves. When they hit
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{L}}}
and
\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}^{U}}}
, then they induce a subset
R_{\varepsilon}\subset\widehat{\widehat{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}}}
. Our wish will be that
R_{\varepsilon}
is a
2
-dimensional manifold, which is transverse to the flow
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{-t}.
Then we will follow
\phi_{X_{E_{\varepsilon}}}^{(-\infty,0]}(R_{\varepsilon})
, which will be only
3
-dimensional manifold. Altogether, we started tracking two
3
-dimensional stable manifolds and continue to track one
3
-dimensional flow invariant manifold. This induces the recursion under the backward-flow. We will use this method in Chapter
6
.

We finish the chapter with one auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 206
.

Let
(\ell,(s_{1},\theta_{1},s_{2},\theta_{2}),(y_{1},\alpha_{1},y_{3},\alpha_{3}),(z_{2},\beta_{2},z_{3},\beta_{3}))\in\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)
, then the vector
\gamma(s_{2})-\gamma(s_{1})
can be represented in two ways as

\frac{\|\gamma(s_{2})-\gamma(s_{1})\|}{\|\gamma(y_{3})-\gamma(y_{1})\|}\left[\big(\gamma(y_{3})-\gamma(y_{1})\big)+O(\varepsilon)\right]

or

\frac{\|\gamma(s_{2})-\gamma(s_{1})\|}{\|\gamma(z_{2})-\gamma(z_{3})\|}\left[\big(\gamma(z_{2})-\gamma(z_{3})\big)+O(\varepsilon)\right].

Proof.

The lemma immediately follows from the geometric picture of
\widehat{\widehat{{\rm ev}_{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}(0)
.
∎

Chapter 6
Multiple time scale dynamics

In this chapter, we will see the dynamics of
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
as a fast-slow system on the configuration space
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}
which in different time scales approaches the fast or slow subsystem as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. If we ignore the singularities at special and diagonal points, the slow subsystem will look like
-\nabla E_{0}
dynamics on
4
sheets of
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
embedded into
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\times S^{1})^{2}
. In addition, the fast system looks like the flow in
\theta
-directions between the
4
sheets, which are now normally hyperbolic critical manifolds (again, the situation is a bit more complicated once also the special and diagonal points get involved). For
\varepsilon>0
small, we will be able to recover the dynamics of the fast-slow system from the combination of the fast and slow subsystems. The Fenichel theory and Exchange Lemmata will be the techniques that we will be using for this task.

In addition, one of the sheets, where the slow dynamics is contained, lies in
M_{K,\varepsilon}.
Hence, we will be able to construct the bijective correspondence between the
-\nabla E_{0}
-heteroclinic orbits on
(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
and the
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
-heteroclinic orbits
M_{K,\varepsilon}
, provided that the rest points have low Morse indices and do not lie on diagonals. In fact, we will be able to show the bijective correspondence also for the Morse flow trees. For the surjectivity, we will adapt the compactness from Chapter
4
, where we did the adiabatic limit with the Conley index. Later, in Chapter
7
, the correspondence of trees will allow us to relate various cord algebras.

We remark that in Morse theory, the Multiple-time scale dynamics naturally appeared in the following places and was used for the bijective correspondence of (broken) heteroclinic orbits. It was used in
[
96
]
for Lagrangian multipliers and in
[
11
]
for the cascades between Bott-critical manifolds.

In Sections
6.1
-
6.3
we recollect the notions of Fenichel theory and Exchange Lemmata. Then, finally, in Section
6.4
we apply the theory to our setup.

6.1
Basic tracking of trajectories

Gronwall’s inequality 207
.

[
54
]
Let
u\in C^{0}([0,T],\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Assume that
C,L
are non-negative constants such that

u(t)\leq C+\int_{0}^{t}Lu(s)ds

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Then, for each such
t
holds that

u(t)\leq Ce^{Lt}.

Remark 208
.

[
54
]
A useful consequence of Gronwall’s inequality
207
is that the flow of
C^{1}
vector field depends continuously on initial conditions.

Lemma 209
.

[
109
, lem 3.2.7]
Let
f
and
f^{pert}
be two
C^{1}
vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let us consider an open bounded set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Assume that
f
and
f^{pert}
are
C^{0}

\varepsilon
-close on
\overline{U}
, i. e.

\sup_{x\in\overline{U}}\|f(x)-f^{pert}(x)\|\leq\varepsilon.

(6.1)

We fix
T>0
and denote by
\phi^{t}
and
\phi^{t}_{pert}
the flows generated by
f
and
f^{pert}
, respectively. Then for each
x\in\overline{U}
and
|t|\leq T
holds

\|\phi^{t}(x)-\phi^{t}_{pert}(x)\|=O(\varepsilon Te^{LT}),

provided that
\phi^{t}(x)
and
\phi^{t}_{pert}(x)
remain in the compact set
\overline{U}
. Here
L
is the Lipschitz constant of
f
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\|\phi^{t}(x)-\phi^{t}_{pert}(x)\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\phi^{0}(x)-\phi^{0}_{pert}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}f(\phi^{s}(x))-f^{pert}(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))ds\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\phi^{s}(x))-f^{pert}(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))\|ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\phi^{s}(x))-f(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))+f(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))-f^{pert}(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))\|ds

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}\varepsilon T+\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))-f^{pert}(\phi^{s}_{pert}(x))\|ds

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}\varepsilon T+\int_{0}^{t}L\|\phi^{s}_{pert}(x)-\phi^{s}_{pert}(x)\|ds

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}\varepsilon Te^{LT},

where in the step
(1.)
we used the assumption (
6.1
), in
(2.)
we used the Lipschitz continuity of
f
, and finally in
(3.)
we applied Gronwall’s inequality
207
.
∎

Remark 210
.

Alternatively, one can use an Implicit function theorem argument and show a smooth dependence of
\phi^{t}(x)
on the initial data. Here, one has to consider the vector fields from a bounded open subset of the space of bounded vector fields with the uniform
C^{k}
-norm. See
[
32
, appx A]
.

6.2
Fenichel theory

From now on in this chapter, we will consider that
k
is a natural number bigger than
3
.

Definition 211
.

Let

\dot{z}=f(z)

(6.2)

be a general autonomous ODE on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by a
C^{k}
function
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
\phi^{t}
will denote the flow induced by (
6.2
). Let
M
be a connected compact
C^{k}
-manifold in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with corners (
M
is potentially of
\codim>0
).
M
is called:

•

Overflowing
if
\phi^{t}(p)\in M
for every
p\in M
and
t\leq 0
, and in addition the vector field of (
6.2
) is strictly outward-pointing from
\partial M
(recall the terminology for vectors on the boundary in Definition
32
).

•

Inflowing
if
\phi^{t}(p)\in M
for every
p\in M
and
t\geq 0
, and in addition the vector field of (
6.2
) is strictly inward-pointing from
\partial M
.

•

Invariant
if
\phi^{t}(p)\in M
for every
p\in M
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

Locally invariant
if for every
p\in M^{\circ}
there exists some open interval
I_{p}
such that
0\in I_{p}
and
\phi^{t}(p)\in M
for
t\in I_{p}
.

Remark 212
.

[
37
]
We would like to inspect the stability of the
overflowing
manifold
M
under a perturbation of
f
. For this, we will first introduce the rate of growth of normal vectors under the linearised dynamics in backward time. And then we will impose a function measuring the flattening of orbits relative to
M
by relating the normal and tangential directions in backward time.

More precisely, let
\mathcal{N}:=N(M,\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the normal bundle of
M
. Let
p\in M
. Then for any
v_{0}\in T_{p}M
and
w_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{p}
we put

v_{-t}:=d\phi^{-t}(p)v_{0}\hbox{ and }w_{-t}:=(\pi_{M}\circ d\phi^{-t})w_{0}.

Now, we consider any Riemannian metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and define the following
generalized Lyapunov-type numbers
as

\nu(p)=\inf\left\{a\,\Big|\,\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|w_{0}\|}{\|w_{-t}\|}/a^{t}=0,\hbox{ for each }w_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{p}\right\}

and, if
\nu(p)<1
,

\sigma(p)=\inf\left\{b\,\Big|\,\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\big(\|w_{0}\|^{b}/\|v_{0}\|\big)/\big(\|w_{-t}\|^{b}/\|v_{-t}\|\big)=0,\hbox{ for each }v_{0}\in T_{p}M,w_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{p}\right\}.

Remark 213
.

[
37
,
109
]

\nu
and
\sigma
are independent of choices of the Riemannian metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{N}
. Moreover,
\nu
and
\sigma
are constant along orbits.

Also, for the computation (especially at equilibrium points), there are useful relations

\displaystyle\nu(p)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}}\|\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\circ d\phi^{t}(\phi^{-t}(p))|_{\mathcal{N}}\|^{1/t},

\displaystyle\sigma(p)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}}\frac{\log\|d\phi^{-t}(p)|_{TM}\|}{-\log\|\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\circ d\phi^{t}(\phi^{-t}(p))|_{\mathcal{N}}\|}.

Theorem 214
.

[
37
,
109
,
39
]
Let
M
be an overflowing manifold for the system (
6.2
). Assume that
\nu(p)<1
and
\sigma(p)<1/k
for each
p\in M
.

Let the
C^{k}
-vector field
f^{\varepsilon}
be
C^{1}

O(\varepsilon)
-close to
f
, where
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
. Then there is an overflowing
C^{k}
-manifold
M_{\varepsilon}
for
f^{\varepsilon}
which is diffeomorphic to
M
. In addition,
M
and
M^{\varepsilon}
are
O(\varepsilon)
-close in Hausdorff distance.

Sketch proof.

The proof is based on the original idea of Hadamard from
1901
of the graph transform, see
[
52
]
. First, we consider the disc bundle
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
over
M
consisting of normal vectors of norm
\leq\delta
. This gives us local coordinates
\mathbb{R}^{\dim M}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-\dim M}
that identify locally
M
with
\mathbb{R}^{\dim M}\times\{0\}
. For simplicity, we assume the coordinates are global. In particular, any section
v\in\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
can be seen as a graph (
=:\text{graph}(v)
) over
\mathbb{R}^{\dim M}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
be the space of sections in
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
with Lipschitz constant
\delta
. Then we define a graph transform
G
on
\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
by

\text{graph}(v)\mapsto\phi^{T}_{\varepsilon}(\text{graph}(v)),

for some fixed
T>1
. If we ignore what overflows from the bundle
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
and
\delta,\varepsilon
are sufficiently small, then
G
is well-defined. Next, the generalized Lyapunov-type numbers
\nu,\sigma
imply that
\phi^{T}
is contracting
\delta
of
D_{\delta}
. However, if
\delta
and
\varepsilon
are sufficiently small, then
G
is also a contraction. Hence, by the Banach contraction mapping theorem,
G
has a unique fixed point
v
. In particular,
\hbox{graph}(v)\subseteq\phi^{T}_{\varepsilon}(\hbox{graph}(v))
. Then an inductive argument, starting from small
t>0
, shows that
\hbox{graph}(v)\subseteq\phi^{t}_{\varepsilon}(\hbox{graph}(v))
for all
t\geq 0
.

In the end, we remarked that we worked in a slightly simplified setting. In order to show the regularity of
M^{\varepsilon}
, we should not have started with
D_{\delta}\subset\mathcal{N}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We should have rather considered
D_{\delta}
inside a so-called transversal bundle. That is a certain
C^{k}

\dim M
-bundle in
T\mathbb{R}^{n}|_{M}
that is close to
\mathcal{N}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n})
(which is only
C^{k-1}
). For more details on the regularity of
M^{\varepsilon}
, see
[
109
]
.
∎

Remark 215
.

Reversing time brings a straightforward extension of Theorem
214
to inflowing manifolds and, in particular, to boundary-less invariant manifolds.

In the case of invariant manifolds with boundaries, the situation is a bit more delicate. By the definition, the vector field
f
is also defined on a small neighborhood
U
of
M
. Now, we deform the vector field
f
by adding certain small bump functions supported in
U\setminus M
. The purpose of this modification is that we will obtain a compact set
\widetilde{M}\supset M
, where
\widetilde{M}
is inflowing or overflowing. The downside is that for the perturbation
f^{\varepsilon}
, the manifold
M^{\varepsilon}
will be only locally invariant due to the potential leaving of the flow through the boundary. Luckily, in our applications, we will be able to recover a lot of the structure of the dynamics restricted to
M^{\varepsilon}
. For more details on this construction, see
[
39
,
108
]
.

Remark 216
.

[
37
,
109
]
Continuing the discussion from Remark
213
, we introduce new
generalized Lyapunov-type numbers
. Assume we have a
(C^{k})
splitting

T\mathbb{R}^{n}|_{M}=\mathcal{N}^{u}\oplus TM\oplus\mathcal{N}^{s},

where we assume that
\mathcal{N}^{u}\oplus TM
and
\mathcal{N}^{s}\oplus TM
are invariant under
D\phi^{t}
from the system (
6.2
) for
t\leq 0
. Let us pick a Riemannian metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now, we put

\displaystyle\nu^{u}(p)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}}\|\pi_{\mathcal{N}^{u}}\circ d\phi^{t}(\phi^{-t}(p))|_{\mathcal{N}^{u}}\|^{1/t},

\displaystyle\nu^{s}(p)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}}\|\pi_{\mathcal{N}^{s}}\circ d\phi^{-t}(\phi^{t}(p))|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}}\|^{1/t},

\displaystyle\sigma^{u}(p)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}}\frac{\log\|d\phi^{-t}(p)|_{TM}\|}{-\log\|\pi_{\mathcal{N}^{u}}\circ d\phi^{t}(\phi^{-t}(p))|_{\mathcal{N}^{u}}\|},

\displaystyle\sigma^{s}(p)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}}\frac{\log\|d\phi^{t}(p)|_{TM}\|}{-\log\|\pi_{\mathcal{N}^{s}}\circ d\phi^{-t}(\phi^{t}(p))|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}}\|}.

These numbers are also independent of the Riemannian metric and constant along orbits.

Unstable manifold Theorem 217
.

[
37
,
109
]
Let
M
be an overflowing
C^{k}
-manifold for the system (
6.2
) such that
\hbox{sup}_{p\in M}\hbox{max}\{\nu^{u}(p),\nu^{s}(p),\sigma^{u}(p)\}<1.
Then there is an overflowing
C^{k}
-manifold
W^{u}
tangent to
\mathcal{N}^{u}
along
M
.

W^{u}
persists under perturbation by
C^{k}
-vector field
f^{\varepsilon}

C^{1}

O(\varepsilon)
-close to
f
. In more detail, there is an overflowing
C^{k}
-manifold
W^{u}_{\varepsilon}
diffeomorphic to
W^{u}
and
O(\varepsilon)
-close in Hausdorff distance.

Sketch proof.

For the existence, we study the graph transform on sections of
\mathcal{N}^{u}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{u}
given by the linearised flow. Hence, the Banach contraction mapping theorem gives us the existence of
W^{u}
in a small neighborhood
U
of
M
.

For the persistence, we do not need to compute generalized Lyapunov-type numbers for
W^{u}
. But we would rather use the fact that in
U
the manifolds
W^{u}
and
\mathcal{N}^{u}
are close (in the coordinates induced by the splitting of
T\mathbb{R}^{n}|_{M}
). Hence, if
U
is sufficiently small, then we obtain the appropriate bounds for the Banach contraction mapping theorem and thus
W^{u}_{\varepsilon}
. ∎

Definition 218
.

Let
M
be a compact invariant manifold without boundary. The splitting
T\mathbb{R}^{n}|_{M}=\mathcal{N}^{u}\oplus TM\oplus\mathcal{N}^{s}
is called
normally hyperbolic
, if

\hbox{sup}_{p\in M}\hbox{max}\{\nu^{u}(p),\nu^{s}(p),\sigma^{u}(p),\sigma^{s}(p)\}<1.

Then
M
is called a
normally hyperbolic manifold
. We will denote by
\ell_{u}
the dimension of fibers of
\mathcal{N}^{u}
and by
\ell_{s}
the dimension of fibers of
\mathcal{N}^{s}
.

Remark 219
.

[
37
]
The Unstable Manifold Theorem
217
extends also to normally hyperbolic invariant manifolds. In this case, we obtain the existence of overflowing
W^{u}
and inflowing
W^{s}
. Moreover, not only
W^{u}
and
W^{s}
persist under perturbation of
f
, but also
M
persists. In this case
W^{u}_{\varepsilon}\cap W^{s}_{\varepsilon}=M_{\varepsilon}
,
W^{u}_{\varepsilon}
is overflowing,
W^{s}_{\varepsilon}
is inflowing and
M_{\varepsilon}
is invariant. We will call
W^{s}_{\varepsilon}
and
W^{u}_{\varepsilon}
the
local stable and unstable manifolds
of
M_{\varepsilon}
, and denote them rather as
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(M_{\varepsilon})
and
W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(M_{\varepsilon})
, respectively. The unions of the images of
W^{s/u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(M_{\varepsilon})
under the flow for all times give us
global stable and unstable manifolds
of
M_{\varepsilon}
.

Example 220
.

Let
F
be a Morse function on a closed Riemannian manifold
(M,g).
Let
X_{F}
be a gradient like-vector field adapted to
F
and
p_{0}\in Crit(F)
. Then, with respect to
X_{F}
, the point
p_{0}
is a normally hyperbolic invariant manifold with the unique local unstable manifold
W^{u,loc}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
(the uniqueness is with respect to the fixed radius of the corresponding disk in
\mathcal{N}^{u}
).

Hence, if
Y^{\varepsilon}
is a
C^{k}

O(\varepsilon)
-perturbation of
X_{F}
, then the unique perturbations of
p_{\varepsilon}
and
W^{u,loc}_{Y^{\varepsilon}}(p_{\varepsilon})
are
C^{k}

O(\varepsilon)
-close to
p_{0}
and
W^{u,loc}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
, respectively.

Let us moreover assume that the perturbation
Y^{\varepsilon}
of
X_{F}
is
C^{k}
smooth in
\varepsilon\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
, where
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is small. We can then study perturbations
Y^{\varepsilon}\times\{0\}
of
X_{F}\times\{0\}
on
M\times[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
. By
[
39
, pg 90]
, the graph transform argument can still be applied in this case. Hence,
p_{\varepsilon}
and
W^{u,loc}_{Y^{\varepsilon}}(p_{\varepsilon})
will vary in
C^{k}
families parametrized by
\varepsilon
. We remark that since all of these manifolds can be seen as
\varepsilon
-parametrized graphs over
p_{0}
and a disc in
\mathcal{N}^{u}
, they are in fact isotopic submanifolds of
M
. We will also use this trick (of augmenting the vector field) in Theorem
225
for the fast-slow systems.

Let
\widetilde{W}^{u}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
be a (compact)
\codim 0
overflowing submanifold of the global unstable manifold
W^{u}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
. In particular,
W^{u,loc}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})\subset\widetilde{W}^{u}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
. Hence we can encode every point
y_{0}\in\widetilde{W}^{u}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})\setminus W^{u,loc}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
as
\phi^{T}_{X_{F}}(x_{0})
for some
T>0
and
x_{0}\in\partial(W^{u,loc}_{X_{F}}(p_{0}))
. Recall that by Remark
210
the flow depends smoothly on the initial data
(x_{0},X_{F})
, because
M
is compact. So the
C^{k}
smooth perturbation
Y^{\varepsilon}
, will perturb
\widetilde{W}^{u}_{X_{F}}(p_{0})
also
C^{k}
smoothly by a
C^{k}
family of
C^{k}
maps. Since embeddings are open by Theorem
25
, we will in fact obtain isotopic overflowing
C^{k}
submanifolds of
M
.

To the end, we remark that the smooth family of
W^{u,loc}_{Y^{\varepsilon}}(p_{\varepsilon})
can also be obtained by Perron’s method, see
[
87
, Apx 1, 4]
.

Definition 221
.

[
63
]
A
Fast-slow system
is a system of ordinary differential equations of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon\partial_{t}\theta&=\varepsilon\dot{\theta}=f_{1}(s,\theta,\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{t}s&=\,\,\,\dot{s}=f_{2}(s,\theta,\varepsilon),\end{split}

(6.3)

where
f_{1}\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
f_{2}\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{m})
, and
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
. Here,
\theta
are called the
fast variables
and
s
are called the
slow variables
. We can equivalently put
\tau=t/\varepsilon
and obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\tau}\theta&=\theta^{\prime}=f_{1}(s,\theta,\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\tau}s&=s^{\prime}=\varepsilon f_{2}(s,\theta,\varepsilon).\end{split}

(6.4)

We will refer to
t
as the
slow time
and to
\tau
as the
fast time
. The
fast-slow flow
will be denoted (equivalently) as
\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}t
or
\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}\tau
.

Definition 222
.

[
63
]

The
slow flow
is the solution of the
slow subsystem
, which is the differential-algebraic equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=f_{1}(s,\theta,0),

\displaystyle\dot{s}

\displaystyle=f_{2}(s,\theta,0)

and will be denoted as
\cdot_{slow}t
.

The
fast flow
is the solution of the
fast subsystem
, which is given by

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=f_{1}(s,\theta,0),

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0

and will be denoted as
\cdot_{fast}\tau
.

Definition 223
.

[
63
]

The
critical set

C_{0}
is defined by

C_{0}:=\{(s,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,f_{1}(s,\theta,0)=0\}.

A subset
S_{0}\subset C_{0}
is called
normally hyperbolic
(for the fast flow) if for each
p\in S_{0}
the
(n\times n)
-matrix
D_{\theta}f_{1}(p,0)
has no eigenvalue with zero real part. We will assume that
S_{0}
is a smooth compact
m
-dimensional manifold (possibly with corners).

Points in
C_{0}
, which do not lie in any normally hyperbolic manifold, are called
singularities
.

Remark 224
.

Note that the equilibrium points of the fast flow are in bijection with the points of
C_{0}
.

Theorem 225
.

[
39
,
59
,
38
]
Let
S_{0}
be a normally hyperbolic manifold for the fast flow and let
W^{s/u,loc}_{0}(S_{0})
be its local stable and unstable manifolds. If
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is sufficiently small, then there are families
\{S_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
and
\{W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]},\{W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
such that:

(i.)

Families consists of
C^{k}
manifolds (with corners) and are
C^{k-1}
in
\varepsilon
(the manifolds in each family are isotopic submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
).

(ii.)

W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})\cap W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})=S_{\varepsilon}
.

(iii.)

If
\varepsilon>0
, then
S_{\varepsilon},W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon}),W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
are locally invariant for the fast-slow flow.

Sketch proof.

We augment the system
(\ref{eqn_fast_fl})
by
\varepsilon^{\prime}=0
for
|\varepsilon|
small and would like to apply Theorems
214
and
217
. The variable
\varepsilon
is not too much of a problem due to the behavior of the vector field in the
\varepsilon
direction. However, slow variables contribute with center-like directions that we need to deal with. For this, we perturb the augmented system by bump functions as discussed in Remark
215
.
∎

Remark 226
.

Due to normal hyperbolicity, we can use the implicit function theorem to write
S_{0}
locally as a graph over the slow variable. We will assume that
S_{0}
can be globally written as a graph
\{(s,h_{0}(s))\,|\,s\in K\}
, where
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a compact, contractible (smooth) manifold with corners.

Then by Theorem
225
the
S_{\varepsilon}
can be written as graphs of functions
h_{\varepsilon}(s)
, which are
C^{k}
in
s
and
C^{k-1}
in
\varepsilon
.

On
S_{\varepsilon}
, the fast-slow flow is given as

s^{\prime}=\varepsilon f_{2}(s,h_{\varepsilon}(s),\varepsilon).

and hence in the slow time as

\dot{s}=f_{2}(s,h_{\varepsilon}(s),\varepsilon).

Observe that as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, the limit of the flow exists and is given by

\dot{s}=f_{2}(s,h_{0}(s),0).

In particular, this family of flows on
S_{\varepsilon}
is
C^{k-1}
in
\varepsilon
. Hence, the study of the fast-slow flow on
S_{\varepsilon}
is a regular perturbation problem in the sense of
[
63
, Def 5.1.4]
.

Remark 227
.

On the first sight, the results from Theorem
225
look a bit weak, since the perturbed manifolds are only locally invariant. But in Remark
226
we already described the dynamics on
S_{\varepsilon}
. However, we still do not understand well the dynamics on the perturbations of stable and unstable manifolds. For this, we introduce a certain flow invariant fibration on
W^{s/u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
. Which, in particular, will show us that
W^{s/u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
are decaying to
S_{\varepsilon}
exponentially and hence, the notation is justified.

Theorem 228
.

[
59
,
39
]
Let
S_{0}
be a normally hyperbolic manifold for the fast flow. Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
be small. Let
\{S_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
be parametrized by
v_{\varepsilon}\in S_{\varepsilon}
, where the parametrization is
C^{k}
in
v
and
C^{k-1}
in
\varepsilon
. Then for each
v_{\varepsilon}\in S_{\varepsilon}
there exists a
C^{k}
manifold
W^{s,loc}(v_{\varepsilon})
(with corners) such that:

(i.)

W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\subseteq W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
.

(ii.)

W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
is
C^{k}
in
v
and
C^{k-1}
in
\varepsilon
.

(iii.)

If
\varepsilon=0
, then
W^{s,loc}_{0}(v_{0})
is a local stable manifold of
v_{0}\in S_{0}
for the fast flow.

(iv.)

If
\varepsilon>0
, then
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
is
positively invariant
. I.e. if
v_{\varepsilon}\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}[0,\tau]\in W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
, then

\big\{w\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}\tau\,|\,w\in W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}),w\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}\tau\subset W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})\big\}\subset W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}\tau).

(v.)

For each
\varepsilon>0
there are constants
C_{s}>0,L_{s}<0
such that if
w\in W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
, then

d(w\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}\tau-v_{\varepsilon}\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}\tau)\leq C_{s}e^{L_{s}\tau},

for all
\tau\geq 0
for which
w\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}[0,\tau]\subset W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
and
v_{\varepsilon}\cdot_{f\hbox{-}s\,}[0,\tau]\subset W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
.

Analogous statement holds for the unstable case, only then in
(iv.)
and
(v.)
we consider
\tau\leq 0
.

Sketch proof.

The theorem can be proven similarly to Theorem
225
by a graph transform argument applied to the augmented fast-slow system, which is deformed by bump functions in a neighborhood of
\partial S_{0}
. However, to obtain a result about the unperturbed system, we need to impose the constraints in
(iv.)
-
(v.)
to be sure that we do not leave the locally invariant manifolds
W^{s/u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon})
.
∎

Remark 229
.

Let
D
be a subset of
S_{\varepsilon}
. Based on the construction of fibrations from Theorem
228
, we introduce

W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(D)=\bigcup_{v\in D}W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v)\hbox{ and }W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(D)=\bigcup_{v\in D}W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(v).

Definition/Theorem 230
.

[
39
,
58
]
Let us consider the
C^{k}
fast-slow system (
6.4
) with
m
-dimensional normally hyperbolic manifold
S_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
. Let
U_{0}
be an open (contractible) subset of
S_{0}
. Then there are
Fenichel coordinates
. That is a
C^{k-1}
coordinate change
\Phi^{fen}
of a neighbourhood of
U_{0}\times\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0})
such that (
6.4
) takes the
Fenichel normal form
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}a^{\prime}&=A(a,b,x,\varepsilon)a,\\
b^{\prime}&=B(a,b,x,\varepsilon)b,\\
x^{\prime}&=\varepsilon\big[h(x,\varepsilon)+H(a,b,x,\varepsilon)ab\big],\end{split}

(6.5)

where

(i.)

(
6.5
) is
C^{k-2}
.

(ii.)

A
and
B
are matrices of the form
\ell_{u}\times\ell_{u}
and
\ell_{s}\times\ell_{s}
, respectively (recall that
\ell_{s}+\ell_{u}=n
). The real parts of the eigenvalues of
A
are in
(\alpha,\infty)
, and of
B
are in
(-\infty,-\beta)
, for some
\alpha,\beta>0
. Also
(a,b)\rightarrow H(a,b,x,\varepsilon)ab
is bilinear.

(iii.)

For each
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0})
, it holds in these coordinates that
U_{\varepsilon}=\{a=0,b=0\}
,
W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})=\{b=0\}
, and
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})=\{a=0\}
. (Here, we were considering
x\in U_{fen}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for some
U_{fen}
diffeomorphic to the original
U_{0}\subset S_{0}
.) In particular, along
W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
we have that
(x(\tau),a(\tau),0)
is a solution iff
(x(\tau),0,0)
is a solution. Analogously for
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
.

(iv.)

If
W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\pi_{a}}U_{\varepsilon}
and
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\pi_{b}}U_{\varepsilon}
are fibrations induced by the canonical projections
\pi_{a},\pi_{b}
, then the flow of
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form})
is a fiber invariant, i.e., the flow maps fibers into fibers. Moreover, let
p\in U_{\varepsilon}
and
p_{u}
be a lift to the fiber
\pi_{a}^{-1}(p)
, then the distance of images of
p
and
p_{u}
exponentially decreases under the backward flow. Analogously, for the fibers
\pi_{b}
, only with the forward flow.

This is provided that
\|a\|\leq\delta,\|b\|\leq\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0},\delta>0
are sufficiently small.

By
B_{\delta,U_{fen}}
we will denote the set
\{(a,b,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{u}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{s}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\,|\,\|a\|\leq\delta,\|b\|\leq\delta,x\in U_{fen}\}
. We also put
\Phi^{fen}_{\varepsilon}:=\Phi^{fen}|_{\varepsilon\,fixed}
.

Sketch proof.

First, the coordinate change. Since
U_{0}
is contractible, we have a normally hyperbolic splitting of trivial bundles
T\mathbb{R}^{m+n}|_{U_{0}}=\mathcal{N}^{u}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{s}\oplus TU_{0}
, which gives locally
C^{k-1}
coordinates
(a,b,x)
in
\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{u}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{s}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. By Fenichel’s theory (Theorem
225
), we can, for each
\varepsilon\geq 0
sufficiently small, write
W^{s/u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
in these coordinates locally as graphs of some auxiliary functions

W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})=\hbox{Graph}(h^{u}(a,x,\varepsilon))\hbox{ and }W^{s}(U_{\varepsilon})=\hbox{Graph}(h^{s}(b,x,\varepsilon))

such that these functions are
C^{k-1}
with a domain on
\{x\in U_{fen}\subset R^{m},\|a\|<\delta,\|b\|<\delta,\|\varepsilon\|<\varepsilon_{0}\}
.

Then we change coordinates by straightening
W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
. This introduce new variables
(a_{1},b_{1},x_{1})=(a,b-h^{u}(a,x,\varepsilon),x)
. The Jacobi matrix of the coordinate transformation has a full rank at
a=b=0,\varepsilon=0
, so the transformation is invertible on
\{x\in U_{fen},\|a\|<\delta,\|b\|<\delta,\|\varepsilon\|<\varepsilon_{0}\}
with
\delta,\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small.

Now the argument is similar for the straightening of
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
. Then it remains to straighten the flow invariant fibers of
W^{s,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
and
W^{s}(U_{\varepsilon})
, which were defined in Theorem
228
. This involves changing the
x
-coordinate by the images of the affine maps given by basepoint maps of the fibrations. Again, the rank argument shows that the transformation is invertible on a neighborhood of
a=b=0,\varepsilon=0
.

The Fenichel normal form follows from looking at invariant sets
a=0
or
b=0
together with the property that projections along fibers intertwine with the flow.

In particular, the obtained map
H
is
C^{k-2}
,
[
38
]
.

The signature of eigenvalues of matrices
A
and
B
follows from the normal hyperbolicity of
S_{0}
and Taylor expansion estimates; see
[
63
, Prop 4.1.1]
.
∎

Definition 231
.

[
104
]
Let
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
be a hyperbolic equilibrium of the slow flow on a normally hyperbolic manifold for the fast flow. Then we put

W^{u}_{sing}(\mathfrak{p}_{0}):=\bigcup_{x\in W^{u}_{slow}(\mathfrak{p_{0}})}W^{u}_{fast}(x)

and call the set as the
singular unstable manifold of
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
. The restriction of
W^{u}_{sing}(\mathfrak{p}_{0})
to the local stable manifolds for the fast and slow flows is called the
local singular unstable manifold of
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
and denoted by
W^{u,loc}_{sing}(\mathfrak{p}_{0})
. We will also often use the term

\widetilde{W}^{u}_{sing}(\mathfrak{p}_{0}):=\bigcup_{x\in\widetilde{W}^{u}_{slow}(\mathfrak{p_{0}})}W^{u,loc}_{fast}(x),

where
\widetilde{W}^{u}_{slow}(\mathfrak{p_{0}})
is a (compact)
\codim 0
overflowing submanifold of
W^{u}_{slow}(\mathfrak{p_{0}})
.

Analogously, we also define the terms in the stable case.

Corollary 232
.

[
39
,
104
]
Let
S_{0}
be a normally hyperbolic manifold for the fast flow. Assume also that
p_{0}\in S_{0}
is a normally hyperbolic equilibrium for the slow flow. Then for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, there are families of normally hyperbolic equilibria
\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon}
and their local stable and unstable manifolds. Dimensions of the local stable (unstable) manifolds are equal to the sum of stable (unstable) directions in fast and slow flows. The families are
C^{k-1}
in
\varepsilon
and describe isotopic
C^{k}
manifolds.

Proof.

Let us consider a Fenichel coordinates around
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
. By Remark
226
and the discussion in Example
220
, the following holds. The submanifold
W^{u,loc}_{slow}(\mathfrak{p}_{0})\subset U_{fen}
extends to a smooth family of
W^{u,loc}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})\subset U_{fen}
. Here
W^{u,loc}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})
are the local unstable manifold for the (slow time) fast-slow flow restricted to
U_{fen}
.

Hence, we can smoothly extend
W^{u,loc}_{sing}(\mathfrak{p}_{0})
to
W^{u,loc}_{{f\hbox{-}s\,}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})
by taking the unstable fibers over the perturbed
W^{u,loc}_{slow}(\mathfrak{p}_{0})
. Note that by the flow invariance of fibers and the exponential decay of solutions in
W^{u,loc}_{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon})
to
U_{\varepsilon}
(Theorem
230

(iv.)
) we obtain that in our case
W^{u,loc}_{{f\hbox{-}s\,}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})
are not only locally invariant but actually overflowing.
∎

Remark 233
.

By the discussion in Example
220
there is also the following extension of Corrollary
232
:
\widetilde{W}^{u}_{sing}(\mathfrak{p}_{0})\subset S_{0}
extends smoothly to isotopic
\widetilde{W}^{u}_{{f\hbox{-}s\,}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})
, where
\widetilde{W}^{u}_{{f\hbox{-}s\,}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})
which are (compact)
\codim 0
overflowing submanifolds of
W^{u}_{{f\hbox{-}s\,}}(\mathfrak{p}_{\varepsilon})
.

6.3
Exchange lemma

Remark 234
.

Let us consider the set
U_{0}
from Definition
230
. Assume that
U_{0}
is rectifiable. Then we can, after a
C^{k-1}
coordinate change, write the Fenichel normal form of (
6.4
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}a^{\prime}&=A(a,b,x,\varepsilon)a,\\
b^{\prime}&=B(a,b,x,\varepsilon)b,\\
x^{\prime}&=\varepsilon\big[(1,0,\cdots,0)^{T}+\widetilde{H}(a,b,x,\varepsilon)ab\big].\end{split}

(6.6)

for
(a,b,x)\in B_{\delta,U_{fen}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{u}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{s}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Indeed, first we rectify the slow flow along
U_{0}
. The Flow box theorem, for instance, can accomplish this. And then we make coordinate changes, as in the proof of Theorem
230
.

By
\phi_{red,\varepsilon}^{\tau}
we will denote the
reduced flow
of
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form2})
to
U_{fen}
(parametrized with the fast time).

Remark 235
.

By Theorem
230
we know how to track the trajectories
u_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=(x(\tau),a(\tau),b(\tau))
of the system
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form2})
that start in the stable or the unstable manifold, i.e. in the set
\{a=0\}
or
\{b=0\}
.

In this section, we will be interested in tracking
u_{\varepsilon}
when
a(0)
and
b(0)
are nonzero. First observation will be that the norm of
a
is exponentially decreasing in the forward time and the norm of
b
is exponentially decreasing in the backward time. This follows from eigenvalue estimates from Theorem
230

(ii.)
, for more details see
[
58
, cor 1]
. We also remark that these are only
C^{0}
estimates for
u_{\varepsilon}
.

In order to have more information about the track of
u_{\varepsilon}
or even some flow invariant manifolds, we will present so-called Exchange lemmata which are based on the technique of
Silnikov boundary value problems

[
100
]
. That is a quest to find solutions of
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form2})
on a time interval
[0,T]
with boundary value problems

x(0)=x^{1},a(0)=a^{0},b(T)=b^{1}

(6.7)

or

x(T)=x^{1},a(0)=a^{0},b(T)=b^{1}.

(6.8)

We can heuristically see the existence of the solution to the Silnikov BVP as in Figure
6.1
, see also
[
56
]
. The Exchange lemmata, derived from the solutions of two Silnikov BVPs
(\ref{egn_first_silnikovBVP})
,
(\ref{egn_second_silnikovBVP})
, give us two different perspectives on how the tracked invariant manifolds can be parametrized. To the Silnikov BVP
(\ref{egn_second_silnikovBVP})
will correspond Exchange lemmata
237
and
239
and to BVP
(\ref{egn_first_silnikovBVP})
corresponds Exchange Lemma
240
.

\labellist
\pinlabel

0
at -8 -8

\pinlabel
a^{1}
at 200 -8

\pinlabel
b^{0}
at -8 200

\endlabellist

Figure 6.1
:
Trajectories of the system
a^{\prime}=a,b^{\prime}=-b,x^{\prime}=0
in the rectangle
\{(a,b)\in[0,a^{1}]\times[0,b^{0}]\}
can be parametrized by
T\geq 0
, where
b(0)=b^{0}
and
a(T)=a^{1}
. A solution can stay inside the rectangle for an arbitrarily long time
T
if it is sufficiently close to the axes.

Remark/Notation 236
.

By
\bm{M}
we will denote a
C^{k-2}
submanifold of
B_{\delta,U_{fen}}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, such that
\varepsilon
-level sets are isotopic
C^{k-2}
submanifolds. We will denote the
\varepsilon
-levels by
M_{\varepsilon}
.
M^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
will characterize the evolution of
M_{\varepsilon}
under the flow of
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form2})
.

The following is a simplified version of Schecter’s formulation of the Exchange Lemma.

(\ell_{u}+1)
-Exchange Lemma 237
.

[
97
]
Let us consider the
C^{k-2}
system
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form2})
and the
(\ell_{u}+1)
-dimensional manifold
\bm{M}
(from Remark
236
). Assume that inside
B_{\delta,U_{fen}}
the manifold
M_{0}
intersects
\{a=0\}
transversally at some point
(b^{0},0,0)
. Let
\overline{x}>0
such that

u_{0}(t)=\{(t,0,\dots,0)^{T}\,|\,t\in[0,\overline{x}]\}\subset U_{fen}

(
u_{0}
is parametrized by the slow time) and
D
be a small neighborhood of
(0,\overline{x})
in
ax_{1}
-space. Then for
\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small there exist
C^{k-3}
functions

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}

\displaystyle:D\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{s}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}

\displaystyle:D\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m-1},

such that

(i.)

\widetilde{b}(a,x_{1},0)=0
.

(ii.)

\widetilde{x}(a,x_{1},0)=\widetilde{x}(0,x_{1},\varepsilon)=0
.

(iii.)

As
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, the functions
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{x})\rightarrow 0
exponentially (in uniform norm), along all derivatives up to order
k-3
with respect to all variables.

(iv.)

For
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the set

Q_{\varepsilon}=\{(a,b,x)\,|\,(a,x_{1})\in D,b=\widetilde{b}(a,x_{1},\varepsilon),(x_{2},\dots,x_{m})=\widetilde{x}(a,x_{1},\varepsilon)\}

is contained in
M^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
. See also Figure
6.2
.

\labellist
\pinlabel

\overline{x}
at 172 749

Remark/Notation 238
.

[
97
]
Let us consider
(\ref{eqn_fenichel_normal_form2})
and the
(\ell_{u}+\sigma+1)
-dimensional manifold
\bm{M}
(from Remark
236
), where
0\leq\sigma\leq m-1
.

Let us assume that

(i.)

Inside
B_{\delta,U_{fen}}
the manifold
M_{0}
intersects
\{a=0\}
transversally in a manifold
N_{0}
(of dimension
\sigma
).

(ii.)

N_{0}
projects to a
\sigma
-dimensional submanifold
P_{0}
of
U_{fen}
.

(iii.)

The vector
(1,0,\dots,0)^{T}
is not tangent to
P_{0}
.

Note that for
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small we can (uniquely) define
N_{\varepsilon},P_{\varepsilon}
that satisfy
(i.)-(iii.)
with indices
0
replaced by
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
.

Hence, similarly to Theorem
230
and Remark
