\bm{a}\mapsto\sum_{\ell=0}^{k_{sw}}[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\bm{a},\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\tfrac{1}{2}}_{2\ell})],

where
[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\bm{a},\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\tfrac{1}{2})]
denotes the boundaries of the disks from the moduli space, which become chains of broken strings after connecting the ends of
\bm{a}
by capping paths to the base point
x_{0}
.

Now, by Conjecture
317
, the dimension of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\bm{a},\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\tfrac{1}{2})
is
|\bm{a}|+\ell
. So,
\Phi_{i}(\bm{a})\in C_{|\bm{a}|}^{string}(\Sigma)
, and
\Phi_{i}
preserves degree.

Let us verify the equation
(\ref{egn_chain_map_holom}).
We need to inspect how the codimension
1
strata of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\bm{a},\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\tfrac{1}{2})
are contributing to
\partial^{sing}\Phi(\bm{a})
provided that
|\bm{a}|=1
. By Conjecture
317
(Theorem
313
), there are four types of configurations:

•

Discs with a
\tfrac{3}{2}
switch. These discs contribute by
[0]
as relative chains, since the normal jet conditions of the discs at
\tfrac{3}{2}
switch are matching with
\partial^{Q}\Sigma
.

•

Discs with a symplectization building which are counted by
\Phi(\partial_{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{a}))
.

•

Discs with a switch of winding
1
along
\mathbb{R}^{3}
which are counted by
\delta_{Q}\Phi(\bm{a})
(this phenomenon appeared already in
[
19
]
).

•

Discs with a switch of winding
2
along
L^{\ast}_{+}T_{K,\varepsilon}
which are counted by
\delta_{N}\Phi(\bm{a})
. Note that here we used the shift in grading of
C^{sing}_{\ast}(\Sigma^{Q_{n}}_{\ell})
by the number of trivial
Q
-strings.

For example,
\mathcal{M}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\textbf{a},2,\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})\subset\partial\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\textbf{a},\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
, so
\dim\mathcal{M}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\textbf{a},2,\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})=1
. Hence,
[\mathcal{M}_{T_{K,\varepsilon}}(\textbf{a},2,\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})]\in C^{sing}_{1}(\Sigma^{Q_{1}}_{2})
. But by the definition we have that
C^{sing}_{1}(\Sigma^{Q_{1}}_{2})=C^{sing}_{0+2-1}(\Sigma^{Q_{1}}_{2})\subset C^{string}_{0}(\Sigma)
.

∎

Now, let us discuss more the relations of
H_{0}^{string}(T_{K,\varepsilon})
to
Cord^{M}(T_{K,\varepsilon})
even though the reader is gently warned that the proceeding discussion will be slightly speculative.

Definition 335
.

Let
T_{0}>0
. Then by
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
we understand the space of paths
\mathcal{H}^{3}([0,t_{0}],\mathbb{R}^{3};T_{K,\varepsilon})
that are outward-pointing from
T_{K,\varepsilon}
at their endpoints.

Next, by
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}\subset\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
we denote the realization of the space
M_{K,\varepsilon}
as a space of
T_{0}
-time chords (with constant speed parametrizations). By
\mathcal{P}^{\Delta}_{K,\varepsilon}
we denote the constant paths.

Remark 336
.

\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
is a Banach manifold with corners. This is followed by similar argument as the proof of Lemma
435
.

Definition 337
.

By
\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}
we denote the energy functional on
\mathcal{H}_{3}([0,T_{0}],\mathbb{R}^{3};T_{K,\varepsilon})
. Next,
\partial^{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
is defined as the subset of
\partial\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
, where
-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}
is strictly outward-pointing via Definition
32
. Then by
\partial_{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
we denote the closure of
\partial^{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
in
\partial\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 338
.

The flow of
-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}
is a “deformation” of
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
to
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}
relative to
\partial_{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
.

More precisely. With respect to the flow of
-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}
the set
\partial_{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}\cup\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}
is a positively invariant exit set for
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
(compare with Conley index pairs from Definition
142
). In addition, for every
c\in\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
there is a
t_{0}\in[0,\infty]
such that
\phi^{t_{0}}_{-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}}\cap(\partial_{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}\cup\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

The lemma follows from the following observations. We know from Remark
439
that

Crit(\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}})=Crit(\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}\cup\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{\Delta}})

and also
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{\Delta}\subset\partial_{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}
is locally invariant under the flow
\phi_{-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}}
and
\phi_{-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}}
has no periodic orbits.
∎

Conjecture 339
.

Let
K
be generic and
\varepsilon>0
small. Let
c
be a path from
\partial\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
. Then
c\in\partial^{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
iff
c
can be reparametrized such that one of the following conditions on normal jets
\sigma
of
c
holds:

(S)

At the starting point we have
\big(\sigma^{(1)}(0),\sigma^{(2)}(0),\sigma^{(3)}(0)\big)\in 0\times 0\times(-\infty,0)
.

(E)

At the endpoint we have
\big(\sigma^{(1)}(T_{0}),\sigma^{(2)}(T_{0}),\sigma^{(3)}(T_{0})\big)\in 0\times 0\times(0,\infty)
.

Note that the curve
c
satisfying
(S)
or
(E)
can be viewed as a
Q
-string in
\partial^{Q}\Sigma_{1}
.

Moreover, the analogous holds for paths in
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}
.

Heuristic proof.

Instead of general paths from
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}
we consider only
\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}
(i.e.
M_{K,\varepsilon}
). For this, we use the results of Section
3.5
, where we investigated the behavior of
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
along
\partial M_{K,\varepsilon}
. We would like to show that the chords
\partial^{-}\mathcal{P}_{K,\varepsilon}^{chord}
are precisely those boundary chords that appear as limits of interior chords while creating a single vanishing spike as in Figure
8.5
.

Let us restrict
M_{K,\varepsilon}
outside of weakly special and diagonal points. Recall that in Lemmata
54
and
58
we described
M_{K,\varepsilon}|_{S_{K}}
as a trivial bundle over the standard set
S_{K}\subset(\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z})^{2}
with a typical fiber as the square. Then
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
is strictly outward-pointing along
\{F_{1}^{[\varepsilon]}>0\wedge F_{2}^{[\varepsilon]}=0\}
. Observe that along this set, the chords have an exterior tangent at the endpoints. Moreover, as the chords are approaching the chord with the tangency at the endpoint, they are additionally intersecting
once

T_{K,\varepsilon}
close to the end-point. As the chords converge to the tangent one, the additional intersection points converge to the endpoint, see also Figure
8.11
left. In particular, the chords of
\{F_{1}^{[\varepsilon]}>0\wedge F_{2}^{[\varepsilon]}=0\}
will correspond to
(E).
On the another hand,
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
is strictly inward-pointing along
\{F_{1}^{[\varepsilon]}=0\wedge F_{2}^{[\varepsilon]}>0\}
. However, along this set, the chords have an exterior tangency at the starting points. Hence, these chords do not belong to the groups
(S)
or
(E)
, see also Figure
8.11
right.

\labellist
\endlabellist

Figure 8.11
:
A visualization of the chords near to the boundary of
M_{K,\varepsilon}|_{S_{K}}
.
On the left:
the chords approaching the chord with the tangency at the endpoint become shorter, and the tangency at the endpoint is exterior. Note that the purple point will converge to the endpoint - compare with the vanishing spikes from Figure
8.5
.
On the right:
the chords approaching the chord with tangency at the starting point become longer, and the tangency at the starting point is exterior.

Now, let us inspect the behavior of chords from
M_{K,\varepsilon}
near the diagonal
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
. From Section
3.4
we know that near
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
the set
M_{K,\varepsilon}
looks like a fibration over
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
with cuspical fibers. Moreover we expect that
-\nabla E_{\varepsilon}
is strictly outward-pointing along both sets
\{F_{1}^{[\varepsilon]}>0\wedge F_{2}^{[\varepsilon]}=0\}
and
\{F_{1}^{[\varepsilon]}=0\wedge F_{2}^{[\varepsilon]}>0\}
. We would like to verify that the chords from these sets belong to the groups
(S)
and
(E)
. For this, see Figure
8.12
.

\labellist
\pinlabel

x
at 0 165

\pinlabel
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}
at 230 105

\pinlabel
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}}
at 190 190

\pinlabel
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\widetilde{c}}
at 250 165

\endlabellist

Figure 8.12
:
A visualization of the outward-pointing chords that starts at some
x\in T_{K,\varepsilon}
and are short, i.e. close to the diagonal.
T_{K,\varepsilon}
is drawn as a graph, where the positive co-orientation is pointing below the graph, i.e., below the graph is the interior of
\nu_{\varepsilon}K
. The blue plane is the plane tangent to
T_{K,\varepsilon}
at
x
. The orange lines describe the endpoints of the chords that emanate from
x
and are tangent to
T_{K,\varepsilon}
at the start/endpoint. The chord
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{1}}
has an interior tangent at the starting point and lies entirely on the tangent blue plane.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}c_{2}}
has an exterior tangent at the endpoint and on its interior lies outside to
\nu_{\varepsilon}K
. The purple chord
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\widetilde{c}}
is intersecting
T_{K,\varepsilon}
also in its interior at the purple point. As
\widetilde{c}
approaches
c_{1}
, the purple point converges to the starting point. Also, as
\widetilde{c}
approaches
c_{2}
, the purple point converges to the endpoint point. Compare with vanishing spikes from Figure
8.5
.

∎

Remark 340
.

On a chain map from
C_{k}^{string}(\Sigma)
to
C_{k}^{M}(T_{K,\varepsilon};\mathbb{Z}[\lambda^{\pm 1},\mu^{\pm 1}])

In the spirit of Lemma
338
and Conjecture
339
it seems natural to take singular chains in
\Sigma
and drag the
Q
-strings with the flow of
-\nabla\bm{E}_{\varepsilon}
till
Crit(\bm{E}_{\varepsilon})
or
\partial^{Q}\Sigma
. In particular,
N
-strings will be translated to the words of
\lambda,\mu
. During this process, we will be performing string coproduct operations. However, it is not known how to bound the number of these string operations.

Therefore, such an obstacle was resolved for the knot case by different deformation of broken strings, see
[
19
]
. Their approach was to shrink
Q
-strings to piecewise-linear and then finally to linear. While keeping the (existing) switching points fixed, such a process will have control over the number of performed string operations.

If we try to mimic the approach from
[
19
]
in the torus case, we encounter the following problem. Let us try to shrink a single piecewise linear
Q
-string to a linear one while fixing the endpoints. During this process, a
Q
-string
s
can become tangent to
T_{K,\varepsilon}
at one of its endpoints. If
s\in\partial^{Q}\Sigma
, then we can successfully forget about the string, since our theories are relative to this boundary. However, this is clearly not always the case. So the precise linearization process remains unknown.

Appendices

Appendix A
Maslov index in
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})

A.1
Linear algebra

Definition 341
.

A
symplectic vector space

(V,\omega)
is a real finite dimensional vector space
V
together with a
symplectic form

\omega
, that is, a skew–symmetric nondegenerate bilinear map
\omega:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

A map
\varphi:(V_{1},\omega_{1})\rightarrow(V_{2},\omega_{2})
is called a
linear symplectomorphism
if it is a vector space isomorphism and
\varphi^{\ast}\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}
.

A basis
\{v_{i},w_{i}\}_{i}
of
(V,\omega)
is called a
symplectic basis
if

\omega(v_{j},v_{k})=\omega(w_{j},w_{k})=0\text{ and }\omega(v_{j},w_{k})=\delta_{jk}.

Remark 342
.

Every
(V,\omega)
has a symplectic basis, in particular
(V,\omega)
is even-dimensional. Also, the choice of the symplectic basis uniquely determines
\omega
. For details, see
[
71
, Thm 2.1.3.]

Example 343
.

(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. Let
(x_{i},y_{i})
be standard (Cartesian) coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, then its basis vectors
\{v^{x_{i}},w^{y_{i}}\}
form a symplectic basis of the
standard symplectic form

\omega_{0}
. Hence, in view of Remark
342
, every symplectic vector space is symplectomorphic to
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
for some
n
.

More explicitly,
\omega_{0}
is given as

\omega_{0}(v,w):=\langle v,-J_{0}w\rangle,

\text{where }v,w\in R^{2n},J_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
, and
\langle\,,\,\rangle
is the standard inner product. Here
J_{0}
is called the
standard complex structure
, because we can identify
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega)
with
(\mathbb{C}^{n},i)
such that the action of
J_{0}
is identified with the multiplication by
i
.

The
symplectic group

Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
is the group of all linear symplectomorphisms
\varphi:(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
.
Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
is connected, see
[
71
, Prop 2.2.4]
.

Example 344
.

(V\times V^{\ast},\omega_{\times})
, where

\omega_{\times}((v_{1},v_{2}^{\ast}),(w_{1},w_{2}^{\ast})):=v_{2}^{\ast}(w_{1})-w_{2}^{\ast}(v_{1}),

for
(v,v^{\ast}),(w,w^{\ast})\in V\times V^{\ast}.

Then the
anti-symplectic involution
on
(V\times V^{\ast},\omega_{\times})
is the map
\sigma_{st}:(v_{1},v_{2}^{\ast})\mapsto(v_{1},-v_{2}^{\ast})
. Note that
\sigma_{st}^{\ast}\omega_{\times}=-\omega_{\times}
.

Definition 345
.

Let
V
be a real even dimensional vector space. Then a
linear complex structure

J
is a (real) linear automorphism on
V
such that

J^{2}=-\mathbbm{1}.

We will write
(V,J)
and call this tuple a
complex vector space
.

Remark 346
.

For any vector space
(V,J)
of the real dimension
2n
there are
n
vectors
\{v_{i}\}_{i}
such that
\{v_{i},Jv_{i}\}_{i}
is a real basis of
V
. In particular, there is a vector space isomorphism
\varphi:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
\varphi^{\ast}J_{0}=J
. For details, see
[
71
, Thm 2.5.1.]

Definition 347
.

Let
(V,J)
be a complex vector space and
\omega
a symplectic form on
V
. Then
J
is called
compatible with

\omega
if

\omega(Jv,Jw)=\omega(v,w)\text{ for all }v,w\in V

and

\omega(v,Jv)>0\text{ for all nonzero }v\in V.

The space of all
J
compatible with

\omega
will be denoted by

\mathcal{J}(V,\omega).

Also, we define the space

\Omega(V,J):=\{\omega\text{ symplectic form on }V\,|\,J\in\mathcal{J}(V,\omega)\},

or equivalently,

\Omega(V,J):=\{g(J\cdot,\cdot)\,|\,g\text{ inner product on }V\text{ such that }J^{\ast}g=g\}.

Remark 348
.

If
J\in\mathcal{J}(V,\omega)
, then
g_{J}(v,w):=\omega(v,Jw)
defines an inner product on
V
and
h:=g_{J}+i\omega
defines a Hermitian inner product.

Lemma 349

(
[
71
, Thm 2.5.5.]
)
.

\mathcal{J}(V,\omega)
is non-empty and contractible.

Proof.

Due to the existence of the symplectic basis, we only need to consider
\mathcal{J}(R^{2n},\omega_{0})
.

Elements of
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
can be characterized by

J^{2}=-\mathbbm{1},\quad J^{T}J_{0}J=J_{0},\quad\langle v,-J_{0}Jv\rangle>0\text{ for all nonzero }v\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

(A.1)

Thus

(J_{0}J)^{T}=-J^{T}J_{0}=J^{T}J_{0}J^{2}=J_{0}J,

where in the first equality we used the definition of
J_{0}
. In the next two equalities, we used consequently the first two identities of (
A.1
).
Observe that

P:=-J_{0}J

is a symmetric, positive definite, and symplectic matrix. Hence any
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
can be written uniquely in the form
J=J_{0}P
, where
P
satisfies the conditions above. Then by
[
71
, Thm 2.2.3.]
from the eigendecompositions of
P
and
P^{\alpha}
we know that any power
P^{\alpha}
is also of this kind for
\alpha\geq 0
. Hence, we can connect
J=J_{0}P^{1}
with
J_{0}
by sending the exponent
1\rightarrow 0
. Note that the obtained path lies in
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 350
.

Let
\tilde{g}
be an inner product on
(V,J)
. Then for
g:=1/2(\tilde{g}+J^{\ast}\tilde{g})
we have
J^{\ast}g=g
. Hence
\Omega(V,J)
is non-empty. Moreover,
\Omega(V,J)
is convex and thus contractible.

Definition 351
.

A linear subspace
\Lambda
of
(V,\omega)
is called
Lagrangian
if
\omega|_{\Lambda}=0
and
\dim\Lambda=\frac{1}{2}\dim V
.

Remark 352
.

Lagrangian subspaces are the largest linear subspaces on which the symplectic form vanishes.

Remark 353
.

Let
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
be transverse Lagrangians in
(V,\omega)
. If
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
is a basis of
\Lambda_{1}
, then there is an unique basis
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\}
of
\Lambda_{2}
such that
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n},w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\}
is a symplectic basis of
(V,\omega)
. See
[
62
]
.

Definition 354
.

A linear subspace
R
of
(V,J)
is called
totally real
if
R\cap JR=\{0\}
and
\dim R=\frac{1}{2}\dim V
.

Lemma 355
.

For Lagrangian and totally real subspaces it holds:

(i.)

Let
\Lambda
be a Lagrangian subspace in
(V,\omega)
. Then
\Lambda\perp_{g_{J}}J\Lambda
for every
J\in\mathcal{J}(V,\omega)
. In particular,
\Lambda
is totally real.

(ii.)

Let
R
be a totally real subspace in
(V,J)
. Then there is
\omega\in\Omega(V,J)
such that
R
is
\omega
-Lagrangian. Moreover, the space of such
\omega
is contractible.

Proof.

Ad
(i.)
: Let
v_{1},v_{2}\in\Lambda
. Then
g_{J}(Jv_{1},v_{2})=\omega(Jv_{1},Jv_{2})=\omega(v_{1},v_{2})=0.

Ad
(ii.)
: Let
\tilde{g}
be any inner product on
R
. It induces an inner product on
JR
by
J^{\ast}\tilde{g}
. This naturally induces the unique inner product
g
on
V
such that
R\perp_{g}JR
. Hence
J^{\ast}g=g
and
g(J\cdot,\cdot)
is our desired symplectic form. Since the space of inner products on
R
is convex, the lemma follows.
∎

Remark 356
.

For a pair of totally real subspaces in
(V,J)
there might not exist

\omega\in\Omega(V,J)
such that they become both Lagrangian.

Indeed, let
R_{1}=\langle(1,0)^{T},(0,1)^{T}\rangle
and
R_{2}=\langle(i,0)^{T},(1,i)^{T}\rangle
in
(\mathbb{C}^{2},i)
. Assume that
R_{1}
is Lagrangian for some
\omega\in\Omega(\mathbb{C}^{2},i)
. Then

\displaystyle\omega((i,0)^{T},(1,i)^{T})

\displaystyle=\omega((i,0)^{T},(1,0)^{T})+\omega((i,0)^{T},(0,i)^{T})

\displaystyle=\omega(i(1,0)^{T},(1,0)^{T})+\omega(i(1,0)^{T},i(0,1)^{T})

\displaystyle=-\omega((1,0)^{T},i(1,0)^{T})<0.

A.2
Maslov index

In this section, we start with the Maslov index for loops in the Lagrangian Grassmannian. Then we generalize this notion to loops in the totally real Grassmannian and paths in the Lagrangian Grassmannian. The section will be finished by introducing the notion of Kähler angles.

Definition 357
.

The
Lagrangian Grassmanian

\Lambda(n)
is the manifold consisting of all Lagrangian subspaces of
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
.

Lemma 358

(
[
6
]
)
.

\pi_{1}(\Lambda(n))\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

First, fibre bundles can be seen as examples of fibrations, see
[
53
, Prop 4.48]
.
Hence, the determinant map
\det
on
O(n)
and
U(n)
induce the following fibration sequences

SO(n)\longrightarrow O(n)\xrightarrow{\det}S^{0}\text{ and }SU(n)\longrightarrow U(n)\xrightarrow{\det}S^{1}.

Also,
U(n)
acts on
\Lambda(n)
transitively with stabilizer
O(n)
at
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and in particular
\Lambda
really has the structure of a manifold. Indeed, for an
\Lambda\in\Lambda(n)
, observe that
\Lambda
and
J_{0}\Lambda
are orthogonal with respect to the standard inner product. Next, if
B,B^{\prime}
are orthonormal bases of
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda(n)
respectively, then a linear map that maps
B
to
B^{\prime}
and
J_{0}B
to
J_{0}B^{\prime}
is a unitary automorphism. Here we used the fact that
GL(n,\mathbb{C})\cap O(2n)=U(n)
. From the ambiguity of the choice of orthonormal basis, it follows that the stabilizer of this action at
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
O(n)
. Hence, we obtain a fibration

O(n)\longrightarrow U(n)\longrightarrow\Lambda(n).

Recall that
\det
of any orthogonal automorphism is equal to
\pm 1
. Hence, we take
\det^{2}
to have well defined map on
\Lambda(n)
that assigns to any
\Lambda\in\Lambda(n)
the determinant square of the unitary automorphism mapping the
\{x_{i}\}_{i}
-plane
\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
to
\Lambda
. This induces a fibration

S\Lambda(n)\longrightarrow\Lambda(n)\xrightarrow{\det^{2}}S^{1},

where
S\Lambda(n)
is the set of
\Lambda\in\Lambda(n)
such that
\det^{2}(\Lambda)=1
. Since
SU(n)
acts transitively on
S\Lambda(n)
with stabilizer
SO(n)
,
S\Lambda(n)\cong SU(n)/SO(n)
. Hence, we obtain the following commutative diagram of six fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SO(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SU(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\Lambda(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det^{2}}

From this, we conclude the following LES of homotopy groups, see
[
53
, Thm 4.41]
:

\dots\longrightarrow\pi_{1}\left(\frac{SU(n)}{SO(n)}\right)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\Lambda(n))\xrightarrow{(\det^{2})_{\ast}}\pi_{1}(S^{1})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\left(\frac{SU(n)}{SO(n)}\right)\longrightarrow\dots

(A.2)

It remains to study
{SU(n)}/{SO(n)}
. Since
SO(n)
is path connected and
SU(n)
is simply connected, the LES of homotopy groups shows us that
{SU(n)}/{SO(n)}
is simply connected and, in particular, path connected. Plugging this to the sequence (
A.2
) we obtain that

\pi_{1}(\Lambda(n))\xrightarrow[isom]{(\det^{2})_{\ast}}\pi_{1}(S^{1})\xrightarrow[isom]{\deg}\mathbb{Z}.

∎

Definition 359
.

Let
\gamma
be a continuous loop in
\Lambda(n)
. Then the
Maslov index of

\gamma
is defined as

\mu(\gamma):=\degr\circ\,{\det}^{2}(\gamma).

Definition/Theorem 360

(
[
62
]
)
.

Let
\Lambda_{1}\in\Lambda(n)
. Then
\Lambda(n)
can be written as the following disjoint union

\Lambda(n)=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1}),

where each
\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
is the subset of
\Lambda(n)
of all Lagrangian subspaces that have
k
-dimensional intersection with
\Lambda_{1}
. It follows that
\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
is a submanifold of
\Lambda(n)
such that

\dim\Lambda(n)=n(n+1)/2
and
\codim\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})=k(k+1)/2
.
Then
Maslov cycle (with respect to
\Lambda_{1}
)
is the following stratified space

\Sigma(\Lambda_{1}):=\overline{\Sigma_{1}(\Lambda)}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1}).

\Sigma(\Lambda_{1})
has the structure of a singular algebraic variety of codimension
1
. Here, lower strata are at least of codimension
2
in
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1})
(specially codimension
1
boundary of
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1})
is empty).

Remark 361
.

Let
\Lambda_{2}
be an element of
\Lambda(n)
. Pick any
\Lambda_{1}\in\Sigma_{0}(\Lambda_{2})
. Since
\Lambda_{2}
and
\Lambda_{1}
are transverse, they can be identified respectively with the
\{x_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane}
and the
\{y_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. Hence any
\Lambda\in\Sigma_{0}(\Lambda_{1})
can be uniquely expressed as a graph
\{(a,Sa)\,|\,a\in\{x_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}\}
for some linear map
S:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
\Lambda
is Lagrangian, we deduce that
S
is represented by a symmetric matrix. In fact, this gives us a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}(\Lambda_{1})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow S^{2}(\Lambda_{2})

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\longmapsto\langle\cdot,S\cdot\rangle,

where
S^{2}(\Lambda_{2})
is the vector space of all quadratic forms on
\Lambda_{2}
.

Next, observe that any smooth curve

\gamma(t):(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\rightarrow\Lambda(n)
, such that
\gamma(0)=\Lambda_{2},

can be expressed as
\{(a,S(t)a)\,|\,a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
. Hence the tangent vector
\overset{\bm{.}}{\gamma}(0)=\widehat{\Lambda}_{2}
can be represented by the quadratic form
\langle\cdot,\overset{\bm{.}}{S}(0)\cdot\rangle
. This gives us a vector space isomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{\Lambda_{2}}\Lambda(n)&\longrightarrow S^{2}(\Lambda_{2})\\
\widehat{\Lambda}_{2}&\longmapsto\langle\cdot,\overset{\bm{.}}{S}(0)\cdot\rangle=:Q(\Lambda_{2},\widehat{\Lambda}_{2}).\end{split}

(A.3)

This isomorphism is well defined, i.e.
Q(\Lambda_{2},\widehat{\Lambda}_{2})
does not depend on the choice of
\Lambda_{1}
, see
[
91
, Thm 1.1]
.

Definition 362
.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\Lambda(n)
be a smooth path. Then the number
t\in[0,1]
is called a
crossing
if
\gamma(t)\in\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
for some
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. At each crossing
t
the
crossing form
is defined as

\Gamma(\gamma,\Lambda_{1},t):=\left.Q(\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\right|_{\gamma(t)\cap\Lambda_{1}}.

Remark 363
.

For any
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
, we could express the tangent space to
\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
at any point
\Lambda_{2}\in\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
as

T_{\Lambda_{2}}\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})=\big\{\widehat{\Lambda}_{2}\in T_{\Lambda_{2}}\Lambda(n)\,|\left.Q(\Lambda_{2},\widehat{\Lambda}_{2})\right|_{\Lambda_{2}\cap\Lambda_{1}}=0\big\}.

(A.4)

See
[
62
, Lemma 10.2.4]
.

Moreover, any smooth path
\gamma
in
\Lambda(n)
is tangent to
\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
at the crossing
t
if and only if
\gamma(t)\in\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
and
\Gamma(\gamma,\Lambda_{1},t)=0
. This motivates the following definition, which can be seen as a certain generalization of transversality.

Definition 364
.

A crossing is called
regular
if the corresponding crossing form is non-singular.

Remark 365
.

Observe that Formulae (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) allow us to coorient
\Sigma_{1}(\Lambda_{1})
using the quadratic forms. Hence, if
t
is a regular crossing for
\gamma(t)\in\Sigma_{1}(\Lambda_{1})
, then the signature of the crossing form is equal to the algebraic intersection number of
\gamma
and
\Sigma_{1}(\Lambda_{1})
at
\gamma(t)
.

Definition 366
.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth path
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\Lambda(n)
with only regular crossings. Then the
relative Maslov index of
\gamma
with respect to
\Lambda_{1}
is defined as

\mu(\gamma,\Lambda_{1}):=\frac{1}{2}\sign\Gamma(\gamma,\Lambda_{1},0)+\sum_{0<t<1}\sign\Gamma(\gamma,\Lambda_{1},t)+\frac{1}{2}\sign\Gamma(\gamma,\Lambda_{1},1).

(A.5)

Remark 367
.

Regular crossings are isolated. Consequently, there is only a finite number of nonzero summands in Formula (
A.5
), see
[
91
, pg. 9]
.

Remark 368
.

There is a natural extension of the relative Maslov index to all continuous paths in
\Lambda(n)
.

Indeed, every continuous path in
\Lambda(n)
is homotopic with fixed endpoints to some smooth path with only regular crossings, see
[
91
, Lemma 2.1]
. If any two smooth paths in
\Lambda(n)
have only regular crossings and are smoothly homotopic with fixed endpoints, then they have the same Maslov index, see
[
91
, Lemma 2.2]
. The rest follows from the Whitney approximation theorem, see
[
64
]
.

Lemma 369

(
[
91
, Thm 2.3 and Rem 2.6]
)
.

The relative Maslov index satisfies the following properties:

(Naturality)

For
\Psi\in Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
we have

\mu(\Psi\gamma,\Psi\Lambda_{1})=\mu(\gamma,\Lambda_{1}).

(Concatenation)

For
a<b<c
we have

\mu(\gamma|_{[a,c]},\Lambda_{1})=\mu(\gamma|_{[a,b]},\Lambda_{1})+\mu(\gamma|_{[b,c]},\Lambda_{1}).

(Product)

If
n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}=n
, then
\Lambda(n^{\prime})\times\Lambda(n^{\prime\prime})
can be seen as a submanifold of
\Lambda(n)
and

\mu(\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}\times\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime})=\mu(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})+\mu(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}).

(Homotopy)

Two paths in
\Lambda(n)
with the same endpoints are homotopic with fixed endpoints if and only if they have the same Maslov index.

(Zero)

Let
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then for any path
\gamma
in
\Sigma_{k}(\Lambda_{1})
we have

\mu(\gamma,\Lambda_{1})=0.

(Loop)

If
\gamma
is a loop in
\Lambda(n)
, then

\mu(\gamma,\Lambda_{1})=\mu(\gamma).

In particular, the relative Maslov index of a loop does not depend on the choice of the Maslov cycle.

Remark 370
.

If
\gamma(t)
, where
t\in[0,1]
, is a path in
\Lambda(n)
, then
-\gamma
denotes the
opposite path

\gamma(1-t)
. Thus
\gamma\ast-\gamma
is a contractible loop and consequently
\mu(\gamma,\Lambda_{1})=-\mu(-\gamma,\Lambda_{1})
for any
\Lambda_{1}\in\Lambda(n)
.

For the simplicity, we will use the following (natural) notation. If
(V,J)
is a complex vector space and
V=V^{1}\oplus\dots\oplus V^{k}
, then the expression
e^{J}V^{i}
means
e^{J|_{V^{i}}}V^{i}.

Definition 371

(
[
28
]
)
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\Lambda(n)
and
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. Then the
Kähler angle

\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in[0,\pi)^{n}
and orthogonal decomposition
R^{2n}=W^{0}\oplus\dots\oplus W^{s}
are defined inductively as follows. Take the Hermitian inner product
h=g_{J}+i\omega_{0}
.

If
\dim(\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2})=r_{0}
, then put

\theta_{1}=\dots=\theta_{r_{0}}=0\hbox{ and }W^{0}=(\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2})\oplus J(\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}).

Then we define
\Lambda^{1}_{i}:=(\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2})^{\bot_{h}}\cap\Lambda_{i}\in\Lambda(n-r^{0})
for
i=1,2
. Next, if
\alpha_{1}\in(0,\pi)
is the smallest angle such that
\dim(e^{\alpha_{1}J}\Lambda^{1}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{1}_{2})=r_{1}>0
, then we set

\theta_{r_{0}+1}=\dots=\theta_{r_{0}+r_{1}}=\alpha_{1}\hbox{ and }W^{1}=(e^{\alpha_{1}J}\Lambda_{1}^{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{1})\oplus J(e^{\alpha_{1}J}\Lambda_{1}^{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{1}).

Then we define
\Lambda^{2}_{1}:=(e^{\alpha_{1}J}\Lambda_{1}^{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{1})^{\bot_{h}}\cap e^{\alpha_{1}J}\Lambda_{1}^{1}
and
\Lambda^{2}_{2}:=(e^{\alpha_{1}J}\Lambda_{1}^{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{1})^{\bot_{h}}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{1}
. Here
\Lambda_{1}^{2},\Lambda_{2}^{2}\in\Lambda(n-r_{0}-r_{1})
. Next, if
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\pi)
is the smallest angle such that
\dim(e^{\alpha_{2}J}\Lambda^{2}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{2}_{2})=r_{2}>0
, then we set

\theta_{r_{0}+r_{1}+1}=\dots=\theta_{r_{0}+r_{1}+r_{2}}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\hbox{ and }W^{2}=(e^{\alpha_{2}J}\Lambda_{1}^{2}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{2})\oplus J(e^{\alpha_{2}J}\Lambda_{1}^{2}\cap\Lambda_{2}^{2}).

Now, we repeat until
\theta_{n}
is defined.

Next, using the Hermitian splitting of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
we define
\theta(\Lambda_{1},,\Lambda_{2})J
as
\theta_{r_{0}+\dots+r_{k}}J|_{W^{k}}
on
W^{k}
.

Then by the
positive rotation of
\Lambda_{1}
to
\Lambda_{2}
by
J
we understand the path

\displaystyle rot\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+]:[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda(n)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{t\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})J}\Lambda_{1}.

Also by the
negative rotation of
\Lambda_{1}
to
\Lambda_{2}
by
J
we understand the path

\displaystyle rot\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,-]:[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda(n)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{t(\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})-\pi)J}\Lambda_{1},

where
\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})-\pi:=(\theta_{1}-\pi,\dots,\theta_{n}-\pi)
.

Finally, when
J
is clear from the context, we can emphasize the angle by saying only
rotation of
\Lambda_{1}
to
\Lambda_{2}
by
\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
or by
\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})-\pi
.

Lemma 372
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\Lambda(n)
and
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. If
\Lambda_{1}\pitchfork\Lambda_{2}
, then there is
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
such that
e^{\alpha J}\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}\neq\{0\}.
In particular, Kähler angle is well-defined.

Proof.

By the choice of the symplectic basis, we can assume that
\Lambda_{1}
is the
\{x_{i}\}_{i}
-plane and
J=J_{0}
. If
J_{0}\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}\neq\{0\},
we can put
\alpha=\pi/2
.

So let us consider the case when
J_{0}\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}=\{0\}.
Hence, as in the Remark
361
,
\Lambda_{2}
is a graph
\{(a,Sa)\,|\,a\in\{x_{i}\}_{i}\hbox{-plane and }S\hbox{ is a symmetrix matrix}\}
.
Moreover

\displaystyle e^{\alpha J_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}(\alpha J_{0})^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2k)!}a^{2k}(-\mathbbm{1})^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2k+1)!}a^{2k+1}(-\mathbbm{1})^{k}J_{0}

\displaystyle=\cos(\alpha)+\sin(\alpha)J_{0}.

Hence,
e^{\alpha J_{0}}\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}
is non-trivial if and only if the system of equations

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\,\,\,\,a=\cos(\alpha)b,\\
Sa=\sin(\alpha)b.\end{cases}

has a solution for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\alpha\in(0,\pi)\setminus\{\pi/2\}
.
This is equivalent to

Sa=\tan(\alpha)a.

(A.6)

But
S
is symmetric, hence has a basis of eigenvectors with real eigenvalues. Thus
\alpha
exists and we are done.
∎

Lemma 373
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\Lambda(n)
and
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
, then
\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+]=-\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{1};J,-]
.

Proof.

Let us show the lemma in the case when
\Lambda_{1}\pitchfork\Lambda_{2}
and
J_{0}\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}=\{0\}
. Then the general case easily follows. Moreover, similarly as in Lemma
372
we can assume that

\Lambda_{1}
is the
\{x_{i}\}_{i}
-plane and
J=J_{0}
. And hence analogously
\Lambda_{2}=\{(a,Sa)\}
.

Now,
e^{\beta J_{0}}\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}
, for some
\beta\in(0,\pi)\setminus\{\pi/2\}
, is non-trivial if and only if the system of equations

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\cos(\beta)a-\sin(\beta)Sa=b,\\
\cos(\beta)Sa+\sin(\beta)a=0.\end{cases}

has a solution for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\beta\in(0,\pi)\setminus\{\pi/2\}
.

This is equivalent to

Sa=-\tan(\beta)a

and hence to

Sa=\tan(\pi-\beta)a.

(A.7)

Now, we compare Formulas (
A.6
) and (
A.7
). If we take a
g_{J_{0}}
-orthogonal eigenbasis of
S
, then eigenvectors generate (as
J_{0}
-complex vector spaces) same orthogonal decompositions of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
for
\theta(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
and
\theta(\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{1})
. And the lemma follows.
∎

Definition 374
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\Lambda(n)
. Then
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
denote the set of all
\Lambda\in\Lambda(n)
such that
\Lambda\cap\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{2}\cap\Lambda_{1}
.

Lemma 375
.

\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
is a contractible manifold.

Proof.

Observe that when
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are transverse, then by the Remark
361

\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
is identified with the space of symmetric
n\times n
-matrices and hence is contractible.

Suppose that
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are not transverse. We take a compatible
J
and the induced Hermitian inner product
h
. Since
\Lambda\cap\Lambda_{1}
is fixed for any
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
, we will restrict ourselves only to the Hermitian complement
(\Lambda_{2}\cap\Lambda_{1})^{\bot_{h}}
. Hence, analogously as before,
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
is identified with the space of symmetric
(n-k)\times(n-k)
-matrices, where
k=\dim(\Lambda_{2}\cap\Lambda_{1})
, and we are done.
∎

Lemma 376

(
[
17
]
)
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\Lambda(n)
. If
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
, then the paths
\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J_{1},\pm]
and
\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J_{2},\pm]
are homotopic with fixed endpoints.

Proof.

Due to Remark
373
we will inspect only the case of positive rotations. First observe that for any
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
the positive rotation of
\Lambda_{1}
to
\Lambda_{2}
by
J
stays in
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
for
t\in(0,1]
.

Since
\mathcal{J}(R^{2n},\omega_{0})
is contractible, there is a continuous family
\{J^{s}\}_{s\in[1,2]}
such that
J^{1}=J_{1}
and
J^{2}=J_{2}
. Since by the Gram-Schmidt process the orthogonal complement depends continuously on the inner product,
\{J^{s}\}_{s}
induces a continuous family of
g_{J^{s}}
-orthogonal splittings
\Lambda_{1}=(\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2})\oplus C^{s}
. In more detail, let us consider the space
\{P\in\End(\Lambda_{1})\,|\,P^{2}=P,P(\Lambda_{1})=\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}\}
. Next, if
P_{J}
denotes orthogonal projection induced by
J
, then
\{P_{J^{s}}\}_{s}
is the continuous family, where
\ker P_{J^{s}}=C^{s}
.

Hence for
\varepsilon>0

small
is the map

\displaystyle\gamma:[1,2]\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda(n)

\displaystyle(s,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto P_{J^{s}}(\Lambda_{1})+e^{\varepsilon tJ^{s}}(\mathbbm{1}-P_{J^{s}})(\Lambda_{1}),

is continuous,
\gamma|_{[1,2]\times\{0\}}=\Lambda_{1}
and
\gamma|_{[1,2]\times(0,1]}\in\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
.

Since
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
is by the Lemma
375
contractible,
any
pair of paths from
\gamma(1,1)
and
\gamma(2,1)
to
\Lambda_{2}
makes together with
\gamma(1,t)
a contractible loop, see Figure
A.1
.

\labellist
\pinlabel

\Lambda(n)\setminus\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\,\Lambda_{2})
at 51 23

\pinlabel
\Lambda_{1}
at 310 -6

\pinlabel
\Lambda_{2}
at 360 430

\pinlabel
\gamma(1,\,1)
at 130 210

\pinlabel
\gamma(2,\,1)
at 470 230

\pinlabel
\Sigma(\Lambda_{1},\,\Lambda_{2})
at 50 350

\endlabellist

Figure A.1
:
The extension of
\gamma
to the homotopy between
\hbox{rot}[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J_{1},+]
and
\hbox{rot}[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J_{2},+]

Hence there is a homotopy with fixed endpoints between
\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J_{1},+]
and
\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J_{2},+]
.
∎

Lemma 377
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\Lambda(n)
and
J\in\mathcal{J}(R^{2n},\omega_{0})
. Then

\pm\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,\pm],\Lambda_{1})=\pm\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,\pm],\Lambda_{2})=\frac{n-k}{2},

where
k=\dim(\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2})
.

Proof.

We will prove only that
\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+],\Lambda_{1}))=(n-k)/2
. Then
\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,-],\Lambda_{2}))=(k-n)/2
can be shown by the analogous computation, and the rest follows from the Remarks
370
,
373
.

First, we would like to show that
\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+],\Lambda_{1}))=(n-k)/2
.

Note that that there is a symplectic basis
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n},w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\}
such that
\Lambda_{1}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
and
\Lambda_{2}=\{v_{n-k+1},\dots,v_{n},w_{1},\dots,w_{n-k}\}
. By the product and naturality properties of the Maslov index we can ignore the part with the constant rotation and consider only
\Lambda_{1}
as the
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-k}\text{--plane}
and
\Lambda_{2}
as the
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-k}\text{--plane}
. Moreover, by Lemma
376
we can assume that
J=J_{0}
.

Hence we have

\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+],\Lambda_{1}))=\mu(e^{tJ_{0}}\{x_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane},\{x_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane}).

Observe that the only crossing appears at
t=0
. Next, let
\varepsilon>0
small, then for
t\in[0,1)
we have

\displaystyle\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+](t)

\displaystyle=\{(\cos(t)a,\sin(t)\,a)\,|\,a\in\{x_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane}\}

\displaystyle=\{(a,\tan(t)\,a)\,|\,a\in\{x_{i}\}_{i}\text{--plane}\}.

Hence, the corresponding crossing form at
t=0
is

\langle\cdot,\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\rvert_{t=0}\tan(t)\cdot\rangle=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

and
\mu(\hbox{rot}\>\![\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};J,+],\Lambda_{1}))=(n-k)/2
by Formula (
A.5
).

The case
\mu(\hbox{rot}^{\,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}_{J,-},\Lambda_{1}))
follows from the analoguos computations. In the remaining cases, we may choose a symplectic basis such that
\Lambda_{2}
is the
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-k}\text{--plane}
(instead of
\Lambda_{1}
) and the rest is analogous.
∎

Definition 378
.

The
totally real Grassmanian

R(n)
is the manifold consisting of all totally real subspaces of
(\mathbb{C}^{n},i)
.

Remark 379
.

Analogously as for
\Lambda(n)
in Lemma
358
we see that
R(n)
has the structure of the homogenuous space. That is

R(n)=GL(n,\mathbb{C})/GL(n,\mathbb{R}).

Let us naively inspect the stratification of
R(n)
. Let
\Sigma_{k}^{real}(R)
denote the subset of
R(n)
of all totally real subspaces that have
k
-dimensional intersection with the totally real subspace
R
. Take
\Sigma_{0}^{real}(i\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then from looking on graphs over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\Sigma_{0}^{real}(i\mathbb{R}^{2})\cong\{A\in\hbox{Mat}_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{R})\,|\,\pm i\notin\hbox{spectrum}(A)\}.

But
\hbox{Mat}_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}
and from the characteristic polynomial we see that the space of
A
such that
\pm i\in\hbox{spectrum}(A)
has the dimension equal to
2
. Hence
\Sigma_{0}^{real}(i\mathbb{R}^{2})
is neither contractible nor even only simply connected.

However, by the Gram-Schmidt process, the natural inclusion
\Lambda(n)\hookrightarrow R(n)
is a deformation retract.

Now we define a map

\displaystyle\rho:R(n)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow S^{1}

\displaystyle[A]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{\det(A^{2})}{\det(AA^{\ast})},

where
A\in GL(n,\mathbb{C})
and
\ast
denotes conjugate transpose. Observe that
\rho
is well defined.

Definition 380

(
[
67
, C.3.]
)
.

Let
\gamma
be a continuous loop in
R(n)
. Then the
Maslov index of

\gamma
is defined as

\mu(\gamma):=\degr\circ\,\rho(\gamma).

Remark 381
.

[
83
]
Maslov index of a loop in
R(n)
is also well defined on homotopy classes of loops. In particular, when
\gamma
is a loop in
\Lambda(n)\subset R(n)
, then Definitions
359
and
380
coincide.

Appendix B
Maslov index of Reeb chords

B.1
Vector bundles

Definition 382
.

An
orthogonal vector bundle

(E\rightarrow M,g)
is a continuous real vector bundle with an
orthogonal structure

g
, that is a continuous assignment of an inner product
g_{x}
to the fibers
E_{x}
, for each
x\in M
.

Moreover, if
F
is a subbundle of
E
, then the subbundle
F^{\bot}
is defined fiberwise as

F^{\bot}:=\{v\in E_{x}\,|\,g_{x}(v,w)=0\hbox{ for all }w\in E_{x}\}.

Remark 383
.

Observe that
F\oplus F^{\bot}\cong E.

Definition 384
.

A
symplectic vector bundle

(E\rightarrow M,\omega)
is a continuous real vector bundle with a
symplectic structure
\omega
, that is a continuous assignment of (linear) symplectic forms
\omega_{x}
to the fibers
E_{x}
for each
x\in M
.

A
Lagrangian subbundle

(L\rightarrow M)
is a subbundle of
E
with fibers consisting of Lagrangian subspaces.

Two symplectic vector bundles
(E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2},\omega_{2})
are
isomorphic
, if there is a vector bundle isomorphism
\psi:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
such that
\psi^{\ast}\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}
.

Next, two Lagrangian subbundles
L_{1}\subset E_{1}
and
L_{2}\subset E_{2}
are
isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism
\psi
of symplectic bundles
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
such that
\psi L_{1}=L_{2}
.

A
complex vector bundle

(E\rightarrow M,J)
is a continuous real vector bundle with a
complex structure

J
, that is a continuous assignment of linear complex structures
J_{x}
to
E_{x}
for each
x\in M
.

Two complex vector bundles
(E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1},J_{1})
and
(E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2},J_{2})
are
isomorphic
if there is a vector bundle isomorphism
\psi:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
such that
\psi^{\ast}J_{2}=J_{1}
.

A complex structure
J
is called
compatible with
\omega
, if
J_{x}
is compatible with
\omega_{x}
on
E_{x}
for each
x\in M
. The
Space of all
J
compatible with

\omega
will be denoted by

\mathcal{J}(E,\omega).
Analogously we define the space

\Omega(E,J):=\{\omega\hbox{ symplectic structure on }E\,|\,J\in\mathcal{J}(E,\omega)\}.

A
Hermitian vector bundle

(E,\omega,J)
is a real vector bundle
E
with a symplectic structure
\omega
and compatible complex structure
J
. Then a
Hermitian structure

h
is the Hermitian inner product on each fibre induced by
\omega
and
J
.

Remark 385
.

[
71
, ch. 2.6]
Note that symplectic vector bundles have local trivializations with fibers
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. This is done by a parametric version of the choice of a symplectic basis as in the case
(V,\omega)
. Then the transition functions are elements of
Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
. Conversely,
Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
-vector bundles uniquely determine symplectic vector bundles up to isomorphism. The analogous statement is also true for the complex vector bundles and
GL(n,\mathbb{C})
-vector bundles, for Hermitian vector bundles and
U(n)
-vector bundles, etc.

Remark 386
.

[
71
, ch. 2.6]

\mathcal{J}(E,\omega)
and
\Omega(E,J)
are nonempty and contractible. Also, if
R\subset(E,J)
is a totally real subbundle, then the space of
\omega\in\Omega(E,J)
such that
J
is
\omega
-Lagrangian is nonempty and contractible.

Remark 387
.

[
55
]
Since we would like to work with various trivializations of pullback bundles, it will be useful to recall the definition of the pullback bundle together with its charts.

Let
(E\xrightarrow{\pi}M)
be a (real) rank
k
vector bundle together with the maximal atlas
\mathcal{A}
and let
f:N\rightarrow M
be a continuous map between topological spaces
N,M
. Then the
pullback bundle

f^{\ast}E
is given as a vector bundle
(E_{0}\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}M_{0})
together with a maximal atlas
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, where

\displaystyle E_{0}:=\{(e,n)\in E\times N\,|\,\pi(e)=f(n)\},

\displaystyle M_{0}:=N,

\displaystyle\pi_{0}:(e,n)\mapsto n.

The maximal atlas
\mathcal{A}_{0}
contains all charts
(\varphi_{0},U_{0})
that are constructed as follows. If
(\varphi,U)\in\mathcal{A}
and
f^{-1}(U)
is non empty, then
U_{0}:=f^{-1}(U)
and

\varphi_{0}:\pi_{0}^{-1}(U_{0})\rightarrow N\times\mathbb{R}^{k},(e,n)\mapsto(n,pr_{2}\circ\varphi(e)).

The analogous construction also holds for
G
-vector bundles with any structure group
G
.

Remark 388
.

Let
E\rightarrow A_{3}
be a
G
-vector bundle and consider continuous maps
f_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
,
f_{2}:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}
and
f_{3}:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{3}
such that
f_{3}=f_{2}\circ f_{1}
. Then

f^{\ast}_{1}f^{\ast}_{2}E=f_{3}^{\ast}E.

Remark 389
.

If
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are two
G
-vector bundles over
M
and
f
is a continuous map
f:N\rightarrow M
, then

f^{\ast}(E_{1}\oplus E_{2})\cong f^{\ast}(E_{1})\oplus f^{\ast}(E_{2}).

Theorem 390

(
[
14
,
103
]
)
.

Let
M,N
be topological spaces, where
N
is moreover a topological manifold, and
f_{1},f_{2}
are two continuous maps from
N
to
M
. If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are homotopic and
E\rightarrow M
is a real vector bundle, then the bundles
f_{1}^{\ast}E
and
f_{2}^{\ast}E
are isomorphic. Moreover, the same statement also holds for any
G
-vector bundles.

Remark 391
.

Based on Theorem
390
we can make the following useful observation.

Let
A
be a manifold that is a deformation retract onto
B
, where
i
and
r
are the corresponding inclusion and retraction, respectively. If
E\rightarrow A
is a
G
-vector bundle then

E\cong(\mathbbm{1}_{A})^{\ast}E\cong(i\circ r)^{\ast}E=r^{\ast}i^{\ast}E=r^{\ast}E|_{B}.

Hence, any trivialization of
E|_{B}
(if it exists) induces a trivialization of
E
that agrees with the first one when restricted to
B
.

In particular, vector bundles over contractible manifolds are trivial (take for
B
a point). Moreover, if the structure group is connected, then all trivializations are homotopic.

Definition 392
.

Let
(E\rightarrow M,\omega)
be a symplectic vector bundle and
\gamma
be a continuous positively oriented contractible loop
\gamma:S^{1}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}=1\}\rightarrow M
, where the orientation of
S^{1}
is induced from the canonical orientation of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
L^{\gamma}
be a Lagrangian subbundle of
(\gamma^{\ast}E,\gamma^{\ast}\omega)
. Take some capping disc
\overline{\gamma}
of
\gamma
, that is a continuous map
\overline{\gamma}:D=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\leq 1\}\rightarrow M
such that
\left.\overline{\gamma}\right|_{S^{1}}=\gamma
.
Next, take a symplectic trivialization
\Phi:(\overline{\gamma}^{\ast}E,\overline{\gamma}^{\ast}\omega)\rightarrow(D\times\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. We define the
Maslov index of

L^{\gamma}

with respect to

\Phi

and

\overline{\gamma}
as

\mu(L^{\gamma}):=\mu(\Phi L^{\gamma}).

The Maslov index of
L^{\gamma}
is
well-defined
if it does not depend on the choice of
\Phi
and
\overline{\gamma}
.

Remark 393
.

Definition
392
can be analogously rewritten in the language of complex vector bundles and totally real subbundles.

Remark 394
.

First, we would like to discuss the well–definiteness of
\mu(L^{\gamma})
for fixed
\overline{\gamma}
. But, since
D
is contractible and
Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
is connected, all trivializations
\Phi
are homotopic. Hence, all
\mu(\Phi L^{\gamma})
are equal.

Moreover, if
\psi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of symplectic vector bundles over
D
, then
\mu(L)=\mu(\psi L)
. Indeed,
\Phi\psi^{-1}
is a symplectic trivialization of
E^{\prime}
and
\mu(\psi L)
does not depend on the choice of the trivialization.

However, to investigate the dependence of the index on different choices of capping discs, we first need to introduce the first Chern number.

Remark 395
.

We take a complex vector bundle over the sphere
(E\rightarrow S^{2}=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,|\,x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}=1\},J)
of real rank
2n
. Note that
S^{2}
is a union of the upper and the lower hemisphere:
S^{2}=S^{+}\cup S^{-}
. Also, the intersection of these hemispheres is a circle
S^{1}
, which can be parametrized with respect to the canonical orientation of
S^{2}
induced from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in
t\in[0,1]
as
(\cos t,\sin t,0)
.

Then we choose
\omega\in\mathcal{J}(E,\omega)
and obtain a Hermitian bundle
(E,\omega,J)
. Next, since the hemispheres
S^{\pm}
are contractible, there are unitary trivializations
\Phi^{\pm}:(E|_{S^{\pm}},J)\rightarrow S^{\pm}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2n},J_{0})
. Restricting to
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, we have a continuous family of unitary linear maps

\Phi^{\pm}_{t}:E_{[t]}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}.
Hence, for any
t\in[0,1]
,
\Phi^{+}_{t}(\Phi^{-}_{t})^{-1}\in U(n)
.

Definition 396
.

The
first Chern number
of the complex vector bundle
(E\rightarrow S^{2},J)
is defined as

c_{1}(E):=\deg\circ\det\left(\Phi^{+}_{t}(\Phi^{-}_{t})^{-1}\right),

for
t\in[0,1]
parametrizing the circle
S^{1}
.

Remark 397
.

From the fibration
SU(n)\rightarrow U(n)\xrightarrow{\det}S^{1}
and the LES for homotopy groups, we have that the map
\det_{\ast}:\pi_{1}(U(n))\rightarrow\pi_{1}(S^{1})
is an isomorphism.

By Remark
386
and contractibility of
S^{\pm}
we obtain that all choices of
\omega,\Phi^{+}
and
\Phi^{-}
are homotopic. Hence, we have a well-defined
c_{1}
for isomorphism classes of complex vector bundles over
S^{2}
.

If
E
is trivial, then
\Phi^{+}_{t}(\Phi^{-}_{t})^{-1}=\mathbbm{1}
and
c_{1}
vanishes.

Remark 398
.

By Remark
386
we can analoguosly define
c_{1}
for symplectic bundles over
S^{2}
.

Remark 399
.

Let
(E\rightarrow M,\omega)
be a symplectic bundle and
f:S^{2}\rightarrow M
an orientation preserving homeomorphism, then we put
c_{1}(E):=c_{1}(f^{\ast}E)
.

Remark 400
.

Let
(E\rightarrow S^{2},\omega)
be a symplectic vector bundle and
L
be a Lagrangian subbundle of
E|_{S^{1}}
. Then the unitary trivializations
\Phi^{\pm}
give rise to the loops
\Phi^{\pm}_{t}L_{[t]}\in\Lambda(n)
for
t
parametrizing
S^{1}
. Now we would like to investigate how are the Maslov indices
\mu(\Phi^{\pm}L)
related?

By the naturality and homotopy property of the Maslov index, we can assume that
\Phi^{+}_{0}=\Phi^{-}_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}^{-}L_{[1]}=\{x_{i}\}_{i}
–plane in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

Next, observe that for each
t\in[0,1]
it holds

\Phi^{+}_{t}L_{[t]}=\Phi^{+}_{t}\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}\right)^{-1}\Phi^{-}_{t}L_{[t]}.

Hence, we can compute

\displaystyle\mu(\Phi^{+}L)

\displaystyle=\mu(\Phi^{+}_{t}L_{[t]})

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\Phi^{+}_{t}\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}\right)^{-1}\Phi^{-}_{t}L_{[t]}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\Phi^{+}_{0}\left(\Phi^{-}_{0}\right)^{-1}\Phi^{-}_{t}L_{[t]}\right)+\mu\left(\Phi^{+}_{t}\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}\right)^{-1}\Phi^{-}_{1}L_{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}L_{[t]}\right)+\deg\circ\det\left(\Phi^{+}_{t}\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}\right)^{-1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}L_{[t]}\right)+2\deg\circ\det\left(\Phi^{+}_{t}\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\Phi^{-}_{t}L_{[t]}\right)+2c_{1}(E)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\Phi^{-}L\right)+2c_{1}(E),

where in the third equality, we used the concatenation and homotopy property of the Maslov index.

Now, we are ready to continue the discussion from Remark
394
.

Remark 401
.

As in Definition
392
we take a symplectic vector bundle
(E\rightarrow M,\omega)
, a loop
\gamma
in
M
and a Lagrangian subbundle
L^{\gamma}
of
\gamma^{\ast}E
. How does the Maslov index
\mu(L^{\gamma})
depend on the choice of the capping disc?

Let
\overline{\gamma}^{+}
,
\overline{\gamma}^{-}
denote two arbitrarily capping discs. They are continuous maps

\overline{\gamma}^{\pm}:D\rightarrow M
such that
\overline{\gamma}^{+}(\cos t,\sin t)=\overline{\gamma}^{-}(\cos t,\sin t)=\gamma(\cos t,\sin t)
for each
t\in[0,1]
(again, here we used parametrizations with respect to the canonical orientation of
D
).

Next, since
\overline{\gamma}^{+}
and
\overline{\gamma}^{-}
agree on
\partial D=S^{1}
, by the Pasting lemma, there exists the following gluing along
S^{1}
. We introduce a continuous function

\boldsymbol{\bm{\gamma}}:=\overline{\gamma}^{+}\cup_{S^{1}}(\overline{\gamma}^{-})^{op}:D\cup_{S^{1}}D^{op}\rightarrow M,

where
(\overline{\gamma}^{-})^{op}
denotes capping disc
\overline{\gamma}^{-}
with opposite orientation. Clearly, there is a canonical orientation-preserving homeomorphism between
D\cup_{S^{1}}D^{op}
and
S^{2}
. Hence, by Remark
399
we get to the situation as in Remark
400
. Hence, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 402
.

Let
(E\rightarrow M,\omega)
be a symplectic vector bundle,
\gamma
be a contractible loop in
M
and
L^{\gamma}
be a Lagrangian subbundle of
(\gamma^{\ast}E,\gamma^{\ast}\omega)
. If for any two capping discs
\gamma^{\pm}
it holds that
c_{1}(\boldsymbol{\bm{\gamma}}^{\ast}E)=0
, then the Maslov index
\mu(L^{\gamma})
does not depend on the choice of the capping disc and is well-defined.

Remark 403
.

If
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are two symplectic vector bundles over
S^{2}
, then a straightforward computation shows that for the Whitney sum
E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
it holds that
c_{1}(E_{1}\oplus E_{2})=c_{1}(E_{1})+c_{1}(E_{2})
(see for example
[
62
]
).

B.2
Basics of symplectic and contact manifolds

Definition 404
.

The pair
(M,\omega)
is called a
symplectic manifold
, if
\omega
is a
symplectic form
on the smooth manifold
M
, that is a closed nondegenerate
2
–form on
M
.

Definition 405
.

The
Liouville form

\lambda
on the symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
is a one form
\lambda
such that
\omega=d\lambda
. The
Liouville vector field
X
associated to
\lambda
is the unique solution of the equation
i_{X}\omega=\lambda
.

Definition 406
.

Two symplectic manifolds
(M_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2})
are caled
symplectomorphic
, if there is a diffeomorphism
\psi:M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
such that
\psi^{\ast}\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}
. Here
\psi
is called a
symplectomophism
.

Moreover, let
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are Liouville forms on
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively. If
\psi^{\ast}\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}
, then
\psi
is called a
Liouville diffeomorphism
.

Darboux’s Theorem 407

(
[
71
]
)
.

Let
(M_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2})
be two symplectic manifolds. If
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are two points in
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
respectively, then there exist two neighbourhoods
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
of these points in
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
respectively such that
(U_{1},\omega_{1}|_{U_{1}})
is symplectomorphic to
(U_{2},\omega_{2}|_{U_{2}})
. In other words, all symplectic manifolds of the same dimension are locally symplectomorphic.

Definition 408
.

Let
H
be a smooth function on the symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
. Then the
Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
associated to
H
is the unique solution of
\omega(X_{H},\cdot)=-dH
. The induced flow of
X_{H}
is called a
Hamiltonian flow asociated to
H
and denoted by
\phi^{t}_{H}.

Lemma 409
.

\{\phi^{t}_{H}\}_{t}
is a smooth family of symplectomorphisms.

Proof.

We would like to show that
\frac{d}{dt}(\phi^{t}_{H})^{\ast}\omega=0
. Then it follows that
(\phi^{t}_{H})^{\ast}\omega=(\phi^{0}_{H})^{\ast}\omega=\omega
.

We compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigr|_{t=t_{0}}(\phi^{t}_{H})^{\ast}\omega

\displaystyle=(\phi^{t_{0}}_{H})^{\ast}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Bigr|_{t=0}(\phi^{t}_{H})^{\ast}\omega\right)

\displaystyle=(\phi^{t_{0}}_{H})^{\ast}\left(di_{X_{H}}\omega+i_{X_{H}}d\omega\right)

\displaystyle=0,

where in the second equality we used Cartan´s magic formula.
∎

Definition 410
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic manifold. Then its submanifold
L
is called
Lagrangian
if
\dim L=n
and
\omega|_{TL}=0
.

Definition 411
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold with two Lagrangian submanifolds
L_{1},L_{2}
. We say that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
intersect cleanly along a submanifold
K
if

L_{1}\cap L_{2}=K
and
T_{x}K=T_{x}L_{1}\cap T_{x}L_{2}
for each
x\in K
.

Definition 412
.

Let
M
be a smooth
(2n+1)
–dimensional manifold. The
contact form

\alpha
is a
1
–form on
M
such that

\alpha\wedge(d\alpha)^{n}\neq 0.

A hyperlane field
\xi
on
M
is called a
contact structure
, if for every point
x\in M
there is a neighborhood
U_{x}\subseteq M
and a contact form
\alpha
defined on
U_{x}
such that
\xi|_{U_{x}}=\ker\alpha\subseteq TU_{x}
.

The pair
(M,\xi)
is called a
contact manifold
.

Lemma 413

(
[
51
]
)
.

A contact structure
\xi
is a kernel of some global contact form if and only if
\xi
is coorientable.

If the contact form
\xi
is a kernel of some contact form
\alpha
, then any nonvanishing function
f
defines a contact form
f\alpha
with
\xi=\ker f\alpha
. Moreover,
(\xi,d\alpha|_{\xi})
is a symplectic vector bundle.

In the view of Lemma
413
, we will be studying only coorientable contact structures. Hence, we can write
(M,\alpha)
as a contact manifold.

Definition 414
.

Two contact manifolds
(M_{1},\xi_{1}=\ker\alpha_{1})
and
(M_{2},\xi_{2}=\ker\alpha_{2})
are called
contactomorphic
, if there is a diffeomorphism
\psi:N_{1}\rightarrow N_{2}
such that
\psi_{\ast}(\xi_{1})=\xi_{2}
. Here
\psi
is called a
contactomorphism
.

Equivalently, we say that the diffeomorphism
\psi
is a
contactomorphism
, if
\psi^{\ast}\alpha_{2}=f\alpha_{1}
for some nonvanishing function
f
on
M_{1}
.

Moreover, if
f=1
, then
\psi
is called a
strict contactomorphism
.

Pfaff’s Theorem 415

(
[
51
]
)
.

All contact manifolds of the same dimension are locally strictly contactomorphic.

Definition 416
.

Let
(M,\alpha)
be a contact manifold. Then the
Reeb vector field
R
asociated to
\alpha
is the unique solution of:

(i.)

i_{R}\alpha=1,

(ii.)

i_{R}d\alpha=0.

The induced flow of
R
is called the
Reeb flow
and denoted by
\phi^{t}_{R}.

Lemma 417
.

\{\phi^{t}_{R}\}_{t}
is a smooth family of contactomorphisms.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of the Lemma
409
. Only here we have

\frac{d}{dt}\Bigr|_{t=0}(\phi^{t}_{R})^{\ast}\alpha=di_{R}\alpha+i_{R}d\alpha=0.

∎

Definition 418
.

Let
(M,\alpha)
be a
(2n+1)
-dimensional contact manifold. Then its submanifold
\mathcal{L}
is called
Legendrian
if
\dim\mathcal{L}=n
and
\alpha|_{T\mathcal{L}}=0
.

Two Legendrian submanifolds are called
Legendrian isotopic
if there is a smooth ambient isotopy between them through Legendrian submanifolds.

Definition 419
.

Let
(M,\alpha)
be a contact manifold with a Legendrian submanifold
\mathcal{L}.
A curve
\gamma_{R}:[0,T]\rightarrow M
is called a
Reeb chord on
\mathcal{L}
if it has endpoints on
\mathcal{L}
and it is an integral of the Reeb flow
\phi_{R}^{t}
.

Next,
\gamma_{R}
is called
pure
if its endpoints lie on the same connected component of the Legendrian submanifold
\mathcal{L}
. Otherwise
\gamma_{R}
is called
mixed (from
\mathcal{L}_{0}
to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
)
, here
\mathcal{L}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{1}
are two different connected components of
\mathcal{L}
such that
\gamma_{R}(0)\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
,
\gamma_{R}(T)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Moreover,
\gamma_{R}
is called
nondegenerate
if
T_{\gamma_{R}(T)}\mathcal{L}\pitchfork d\phi_{R}^{T}T_{\gamma_{R}(0)}\mathcal{L}
in
\xi_{\gamma_{R}(T)}
.

Remark 420
.

Let
\gamma_{R}
be a pure Reeb chord on
\mathcal{L}
. Then there is a continuous (positively oriented) loop
\gamma:S^{1}\rightarrow M
, parametrized by
t\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}=S^{1}
, that satisfies the following:

(i.)

if
t\in[0,1/3],
then
\gamma(t)=c(3t)
, where
c
is a continuous “capping” path
c:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
with
c(0)=\gamma_{R}(T)
and
c(1)=\gamma_{R}(0)
,

(ii.)

if
t\in[1/3,2/3],
then
\gamma(t)=\gamma_{R}(3Tt-T)
,

(iii.)

if
t\in[2/3,1],
then
\gamma(t)=\gamma_{R}(T)
.

We take the pullback bundle
(\gamma^{\ast}\xi,\gamma^{\ast}(d\alpha|_{\xi}))
. Next, we define its Lagrangian subbundle
L^{\gamma}
as follows (see also Figure
B.1
):

(i.)

for
t\in[0,1/3]
we put
L^{\gamma}_{[t]}:=T_{\gamma(t)}\mathcal{L}

(ii.)

for
t\in(1/3,2/3]
we put
L^{\gamma}_{[t]}:=d\phi_{R}^{(3Tt-T)}T_{\gamma_{R}(0)}\mathcal{L}

(iii.)

for
t\in(2/3,1)
we put
L^{\gamma}_{[t]}:=\hbox{rot}\>\![d\phi_{R}^{T}T_{\gamma_{R}(0)}\mathcal{L},T_{\gamma_{R}(T)}\mathcal{L};J,+](3t-2)
, where
J
is a complex structure in
\mathcal{J}(\xi_{\gamma_{R}(T)},d\alpha|_{\xi_{\gamma_{R}(T)}})
.

\labellist
\pinlabel

\mathcal{L}
at 500 440

\pinlabel
c
at 60 120

\pinlabel
\gamma_{R}
at 460 190

\pinlabel
\overline{\gamma}
at 270 200

\endlabellist

Figure B.1
:
The Lagrangian subbundle
L^{\gamma}
of
\gamma^{\xi}
(in blue) and some capping disc
\overline{\gamma}
of
\gamma
(in grey).

Definition 421
.

Let
\gamma_{R}
and
L^{\gamma}
are as in Remark
420
. Then the
Maslov index (or degree) of the pure Reeb chord

\gamma_{R}
is defined as

|\gamma_{R}|=\mu(L^{\gamma})-1.

We say that
|\gamma_{R}|
is
well-defined
, if
\mu(L^{\gamma})
is well-defined in the sense of Definition
392
and moreover
\mu(L^{\gamma})
does not depend on the choices of the capping path
c
and the complex structure
J
.

Remark 422
.

From Lemma
376
we obtain that the Maslov index of a pure Reeb chord does not depend on
J
.

B.3
On cotangent bundles

Example 423
.

A classical example of the symplectic manifold is a
cotangent bundle

(T^{\ast}N\xrightarrow{\pi}N)
. To see this, we introduce first a local coordinates
(q^{i}\circ\pi,p_{i})_{i}
on
T^{\ast}U\subset T^{\ast}N
. Let
(q^{i})_{i}
be local coordinates on an open neighborhood
U\subset N
. Then
(p_{i})_{i}
are defined as follows. If
x\in T^{\ast}U
, then
\{dq^{i}|_{q}\}_{i}
form a basis of
T_{q}^{\ast}N
for
q=\pi(x)
. Therefore there are unique functions
p_{i}:x\mapsto x(\partial_{q^{i}}|_{q})
. For simplicity, we will write
q^{i}:=q^{i}\circ\pi
. Also, we will denote points of
T^{\ast}N
as
x=(q,p)
.

Then we will define on
T^{\ast}N
a
1
-form
\lambda
. For each
x\in T^{\ast}N
, the linear map
\lambda_{x}:T_{x}T^{\ast}N\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by
\lambda_{x}:=x\circ d\pi
. It will be useful to have a description of
\lambda
in local coordinates. Since
d\pi(x)\partial_{q^{i}}|_{x}=\partial_{q^{i}}|_{q}
and
d\pi(x)\partial_{p_{i}}|_{x}=0
, we conclude that
\lambda=\sum_{i}p_{i}dq^{i}
.

Now, we put
\omega=d\lambda
, which can be locally written as
\omega=\sum_{i}dp_{i}\wedge dq^{i}
. Since
\omega
is closed and nondegenerate, it is a symplectic form and
\lambda
is a Liouville form on
T^{\ast}N
.

Remark 424
.

Since the cotangent bundle
T^{\ast}N
has the global Liouville form
\lambda
, we can introduce a global Liouville vector field
X
, which is locally given by
X=\sum_{i}p_{i}\partial_{p_{i}}
.

Let
g
be a Riemannian metric on
N
. Then we define a function
H
on
T^{\ast}N
as

H(x):=(1/2)||\,x^{\sharp}\,||_{g}^{2}.

(B.1)

The corresponding Hamiltonian vector field can be expressed as follows. Let
x\in T^{\ast}N
, then we take a normal coordinates
(q^{i})_{i}
centered at
q
and induced coordinates
(p_{i})_{i}
. Then
(X_{H})_{x}=\sum_{i}p_{i}(x)\partial_{q^{i}}|_{x}
.

The only Hamiltonian function on
T^{\ast}N
we will consider is the function (
B.1
).

Definition 425
.

Let
Q
be a submanifold without boundary in
N
. Then the
conormal bundle of
Q
is defined as

L^{\ast}Q:=\{x=(q,p)\in T^{\ast}N\,|\,q\in Q,x(v)=0\text{ for all }v\in T_{q}Q\}.

Remark 426
.

Since
TL^{\ast}Q
is subbundle of
TT^{\ast}N|_{L^{\ast}Q}
and
\pi(T^{\ast}N|_{Q})=Q
, we have a projection along fibers
d\pi(TL^{\ast}Q)=TQ
. Hence, on
TL^{\ast}Q
vanish the Liouville form
\lambda
. Since also
\dim L^{\ast}Q=\dim T^{\ast}N/2
,
L^{\ast}Q
is a Lagrangian submanifold of
T^{\ast}N
.

Definition 427
.

The
unit cotangent bundle
on
(N,g)
is defined as

S^{\ast}N:=\{x\in T^{\ast}N\,|\,\,||\,x^{\sharp}\,||_{g}=1\}.

Moreover, let
Q
be a submanifold without boundary in
N
. Then the
unit conormal bundle of
Q
is defined as

\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q:=S^{\ast}N\cap L^{\ast}Q.

Remark 428
.

Note that
S^{\ast}N\subseteq T^{\ast}N
is a contact manifold with a contact structure
\xi=\ker\lambda_{1}
, where
\lambda_{1}:=\lambda|_{S^{\ast}N}
. Indeed, by coordinate description of
X
, we see that
X
is transverse to
S^{\ast}N
. Hence
\lambda_{1}\wedge(d\lambda_{1})^{n-1}=(1/n)i_{X}(d\lambda)^{n}\neq 0
on
S^{\ast}N
, where
n=\dim N
.

At each point
x\in S^{\ast}N
, we take a coordinate description of
X_{H}
as in Remark
424
. Observe that
X_{H}|_{x}
satisfies equations for the Reeb vector field on
S^{\ast}N
. And hence
X_{H}|_{S^{\ast}N}=R
.

Thus, we conclude the following. Let
\phi_{H}^{t}
and
\phi_{R}^{t}
be Hamiltonian and Reeb flows on
T^{\ast}N
and
S^{\ast}N
respectively. Then

\phi_{H}^{t}|_{S^{\ast}N}=\phi_{R}^{t},

(B.2)

and hence there is a bijection between integral curves of the Hamiltonian flow
\phi^{t}_{H}
on
S^{\ast}N\subset T^{\ast}N
and integral curves of the Reeb flow
\phi^{t}_{R}
. These curves are called
corresponding
.

Remark 429
.

From the coordinate descriptions of
R
and
X
we deduce the following. At each point
x
of
S^{\ast}N
and any submanifold
L^{\ast}Q
of
T^{\ast}N
, we have two splittings:

\displaystyle T_{x}T^{\ast}N

\displaystyle=\langle X_{x}\rangle\times\langle R_{x}\rangle\times\xi_{x},

\displaystyle T_{x}L^{\ast}Q

\displaystyle=\langle X_{x}\rangle\times T_{x}\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q.

By the Lemma
413
,
(\xi,\omega|_{\xi})
is a symplectic vector bundle. By looking on coordinates, we see that
X_{x}
and
R_{x}
form a symplectic basis of
(\eta_{x}:=\langle X_{x}\rangle\times\langle R_{x}\rangle,\omega|_{\eta_{x}})
. This extends to the symplectic vector bundle
(\eta,\omega|_{\eta})
.

Also
\langle X_{x}\rangle,\langle R_{x}\rangle
and
T_{x}\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q
are Lagrangian subspaces when restricted to
\eta_{x}
and
\xi_{x}
, respectively. And analogously these subspaces extend to the Lagrangian subbundles
\langle X\rangle,\langle R\rangle
and
T\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q
of
\eta
and
\xi
respectively.

Remark 430
.

Let
x,y\in S^{\ast}N
such that
y=\phi_{H}^{t}(x)
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then by Formula (
B.2
) we have
d\phi_{H}^{t}(x)R_{x}=R_{y}
and
d\phi_{H}^{t}(x)\xi_{x}=\xi_{y}
.

Moreover

d\phi_{H}^{t}(x)X_{x}=X_{y}+tR_{y}.

(B.3)

This is immediate when
N=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, since then
\phi^{t}_{H}(q,p)=(q+tp,p)
. In the general case, it is convenient to do the computation in the symplectization
\mathbb{R}\times S^{\ast}N
, see also the computation in Remark
295
which works for any
N
.

Remark 431
.

[
51
]
As a contact manifold,
S^{\ast}N
can be identified with the space of cooriented contact elements of
N
, which has a natural contact structure. In particular, different choices of
g
on
N
give us contactomorphic
S^{\ast}N
.

Example 432
.

T^{\ast}S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
is a contact manifold with a contact form
dt-\lambda|_{S^{\ast}S^{n-1}},
where
t
denotes
\mathbb{R}
-direction and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is equipped with the standard inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.

Then
T^{\ast}S^{n-1}
can be writen as

T^{\ast}S^{n-1}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|a|=1,\langle a,b\rangle=0\}.

Now, we can define a map

\displaystyle\psi:S^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow T^{\ast}S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(q,p)

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,q-\langle q,p\rangle p,\langle q,p\rangle),

where
(q,p)
is written with respect to the standard coordinates on
T^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Here
q-\langle q,p\rangle p
is an orthogonal projection of the vector
q_{p}
to
T_{p}^{\ast}S^{n-1}
.
\langle q,p\rangle
is then an orthogonal projection of
q_{p}
to
N_{p}^{\ast}S^{n-1}
.

It follows that
\psi^{\ast}(dt-\lambda|_{S^{\ast}S^{n-1}})=\lambda|_{S^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{n}},
hence it is a (strict) contactomorphism.

Remark 433
.

[
40
]
By the construction, a smooth ambient isotopy between two submanifolds
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in N
induces a Legendrian isotopy between
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q_{2}\in S^{\ast}N.

Remark 434
.

[
40
]
Let
N
be oriented manifold and
Q
be a
\codim 1
oriented connected submanifold of
N
. Then
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}Q
has two connected components
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{+}Q
and
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{-}Q
. Both are diffeomorphic to
Q
. Here
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{+}Q
is determined by the positive co-orientation of
Q
.

Naturally,
L^{\ast}_{+}Q
and
L^{\ast}_{-}Q
will be two Lagrangian submanifolds with boundary
Q
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{\pm}Q\subset L^{\ast}_{\pm}Q
and
L^{\ast}_{+}Q\cup_{Q}L^{\ast}_{-}Q=L^{\ast}Q.

B.4
Binormal and Hamiltonian chords

In this section, we would like to describe relations between binormal and Hamiltonian chords. We begin with the discussion of the Morse index of binormal chords. Then we describe the splitting of
TT^{\ast}N
into the horizontal and vertical parts. The section will be finished by introducing the Maslov index for Hamiltonian chords.

Lemma 435
.

Let
Q
be a submanifold of
(N,g)
and
T>0
. Then the set

\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q):=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N)\,|\,(\gamma(0),\gamma(T))\in Q\times Q\}

is a smooth Hilbert manifold and at
\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)
the tangent space is

T_{\gamma}\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)=\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],\gamma^{\ast}TN)\,|\,(v(0),v(T))\in T_{(\gamma(0),\gamma(T))}(Q\times Q)\}.

Sketch proof.

For details
[
61
]
or in more general setting
[
35
]
. Let
\dim N=n
and
\dim Q=k
.

First, recall the following general fact. If
f:U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a diffeomorphism and
\gamma:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is of class
\mathcal{H}^{1}
, then
f\circ\gamma:[0,T]\rightarrow U^{\prime}
is also of class
\mathcal{H}^{1}
.

Let us pick a curve
\gamma\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)
. We would like to construct a chart around
\gamma
. First, we take a trivialization
\Phi:\gamma^{\ast}TN\rightarrow[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\Phi(T_{\gamma(0)}Q)=\Phi(T_{\gamma(T)}Q)=\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Now, we pick an auxiliary Riemannian metric
\widetilde{g}
on
N
. For the simplification,
\widetilde{g}
will have the property that some
neighbourhoods
of
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(T)
in
Q
are totally geodesic in
N
. Observe that such a
\widetilde{g}
always exists. Indeed, first we pick a chart on
N
around
u(0)
that maps
Q
to some vector space. Then the pullback of the Euclidean metric is locally our desired
\widetilde{g}
. Next, we make the same for
u(T)
. Finally, outside some small neighbourhoods of
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(T)
in
N
we interpolate the pullback metric with any Riemannian metric on
N
, and hence we obtain
\widetilde{g}
on the whole
N
.

Let
V\subset\gamma^{\ast}TN
be a neighbourhood of the zero section such that the map
\exp^{\widetilde{g}}:T_{\gamma(t)}N\subset V\rightarrow N
is a diffeomorphism onto its image for each
t\in[0,T]
.

Now we put

\displaystyle U_{\gamma}:=\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})\,|\,(t,v(t))\in\Phi(V)\hbox{ for each }t\in[0,T

\displaystyle],

\displaystyle\Phi(v(0))=\Phi(v(T))=\mathbb{R}^{k}

\displaystyle\}.

Observe that
U_{\gamma}
is an open subset in

\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})\,|\,v(0)=v(T)=\mathbb{R}^{k}\}.

The latter is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})
and hence also a Hilbert space.

Now the charts

\displaystyle\varphi_{\gamma}:U_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\longmapsto\exp^{\widetilde{g}}(\Phi^{-1}(v))

define a smooth atlas on
\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)
. Moreover, the induced structure does not depend on
\widetilde{g}
.∎

Definition 436
.

We define the
energy functional

\bm{E}
on
\mathcal{H}_{1}([0,T],N;Q)
as

\bm{E}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{T}||\dot{\gamma}(t)||_{g}^{2}dt.

Definition 437
.

A (non-constant) curve
\gamma_{M}:[0,T]\rightarrow(N,g)
is called a
binormal chord on
Q
if it is a geodesic with endpoints at
Q
and at these endpoints
\gamma_{M}
is perpendicular to
Q
.

Remark 438
.

[
61
]

\bm{E}
is well defined. That is,
\bm{E}
does not depend on the choice of charts on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Moreover,
\bm{E}
is smooth.

Remark 439
.

[
61
]
Critical points of
\bm{E}
are precisely the binormal chords and constant paths on
Q
.

Definition 440
.

If
\gamma_{M}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)
is a critical point of
\bm{E}
, then the
Morse index of

\gamma_{M}
is defined as

\displaystyle\hbox{Ind}_{\gamma_{M}}:=\sup\{\dim V\,|

\displaystyle\,V\hbox{ linear subspace of }T_{\gamma_{M}}\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)\hbox{ on which }

\displaystyle\hbox{the symmetric bilinear form}\ D^{2}E(\gamma_{M})\hbox{ is negative definite}\}.

Moreover,
\gamma_{M}
is called
nondegenerate
if

\displaystyle 0=\sup\{\dim V\,|

\displaystyle\,V\hbox{ linear subspace of }T_{\gamma_{M}}\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)

\displaystyle\hbox{ such that }V\hbox{ is a }0\hbox{-eigenspace of }D^{2}E(\gamma_{M})\}.

Morse Index Theorem 441

(
[
5
,
61
,
13
]
)
.

Let
\gamma_{M}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,T],N;Q)
be a binormal chord. Then

\hbox{Ind}_{\gamma_{M}}=index(H_{0}-H_{T})+\sum_{0<t<T}\{\hbox{multiplicity of the conjugate point }\gamma_{M}(t)\},

where
H_{0},H_{T}
are the second fundamental forms of
Q
at
\gamma_{M}(0),\gamma_{M}(T),
respectively, in directions of the normal vectors
\dot{\gamma}_{M}(0),\dot{\gamma}_{M}(T)
, respectively. For definitions of the second fundamental form and the multiplicity of a conjugate point, see
[
61
]
.

Definition 442
.

A curve
\gamma_{H}:[0,T]\rightarrow T^{\ast}N
is called a
Hamiltonian chord on
L^{\ast}Q
if it has endpoints at
L^{\ast}Q
and it is an integral curve of the Hamiltonian flow
\phi_{H}^{t}
.

Moreover, a Hamiltonian chord
\gamma_{H}
is called
nondegenerate
if
T_{\gamma_{H}(T)}L^{\ast}Q\pitchfork d\phi_{H}^{T}T_{\gamma_{H}(0)}L^{\ast}Q
in
T_{\gamma_{H}(T)}T^{\ast}N
.

Lemma 443

(
[
62
, Thm 2.3.1]
)
.

If
\gamma_{M}(t)
is a binormal chord on
Q
, then the map
t\mapsto(\gamma_{M}(t),\dot{\gamma}_{M}(t)^{\flat})\in T^{\ast}N
defines a Hamiltonian chord
\gamma_{H}(t)
on
L^{\ast}Q
. Moreover, such an assignment defines a bijection between the sets of binormal chords on
Q
and Hamiltonian chords on
L^{\ast}Q
. These chords are called
corresponding
.

Remark 444
.

For details see
[
102
, Apx 3]
. Let
(E\xrightarrow{\pi}N)
be a vector bundle. Applying the tangent functor
T
, we get a vector bundle
(TE\xrightarrow{d\pi}TN)
.

The
Vertical subbundle
is the subbundle of
TE
defined as
T^{v}E:=\ker d\pi
. For any
q\in N
and
x\in E_{q}
we define a map
I_{x}^{v}
as

\displaystyle I_{x}^{v}:E_{q}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow T_{x}E

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}(x+t\widetilde{x}).

Observe that
I_{x}^{v}
is a vector space isomorphism onto its image, which is
T^{v}_{x}E
. Hence, we obtain a vector bundle isomorphism
I^{v}:\pi^{\ast}(E)\rightarrow T^{v}E
.

A
Horizontal subbundle
is a choice of a subbundle
T^{h}E
of
TE
such that
TE=T^{v}E\oplus T^{h}E
. Take a Koszul connection
\nabla:\Gamma(E)\rightarrow\Gamma(T^{\ast}N\otimes E)
. Then for any
q\in N,x\in E_{q}
we define a map

\displaystyle I_{x}^{h}:T_{q}N

\displaystyle\longrightarrow T_{x}E

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\longmapsto ds(q)v-(\nabla_{v}s)(q),

where
s
is a section
s:N\rightarrow E
such that
s(q)=x
and the map
I^{h}_{x}
does not depend on the choice of
s
. This leads to the vector bundle monomorphism
I^{h}:\pi^{\ast}(TN)\rightarrow TE
, where the image of
I^{h}
is a horizontal subbundle. Moreover, every horizontal subbundle
T^{h}E
is determined by the unique Kozsul connection on
E
.

Hence, we obtain a vector bundle isomorphism
I=I^{h}\times I^{v}:\pi^{\ast}(TN\oplus E)\rightarrow TE
.

By
P=P^{h}\times P^{v}:TE\rightarrow\pi^{\ast}(TN\oplus E)
we will denote the inverse map to
I
.

Remark 445
.

In our special case we have a vector bundle
(E\rightarrow N)=(T^{\ast}N\rightarrow N)
with a symplectic form
\omega
. Let
g
be a Riemannian metric on
N
. Fix a point
x=(q,p)\in T^{\ast}N
. Now, we take a normal coordinates
(q^{i})_{i}
centered at
q
and induced coordinates
(p_{i})_{i}
. Then

\{\partial_{q^{i}}|_{x},-\partial_{p_{i}}|_{x}\}_{i}
is a symplectic basis of
(T_{x}T^{\ast}N,\omega_{x})
. Next,
g_{q}
induce dual pairing on
T_{q}N\times T_{q}^{\ast}N
. As in Example
344
, this give us a symplectic form
(\omega_{\times})_{q}
on
T_{q}N\times T_{q}^{\ast}N
with a symplectic basis
\{\partial_{q^{i}}|_{q},-dq^{i}|_{q}\}_{i}
.

Moreover,
g_{q}
induce on
(T_{q}N\times T_{q}^{\ast}N,(\omega_{\times})_{q})
a Hermitian structure, where the compatible complex structure
(J^{g})_{q}
and the inner product
(g^{D})_{q}
are

(J^{g})_{q}=\begin{pmatrix}0&g_{q}^{\ast}\\
-g_{q}&0\end{pmatrix}\hbox{ and }(g^{D})_{q}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{q}&0\\
0&g_{q}^{\ast}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.4)

Here
(\omega_{\times})_{q}=(g^{D})_{q}((J^{g})_{q}\cdot,\cdot)
.

We would like to describe
I_{x}
with respect to the bases above. For this, we choose
\nabla
as the dual connection to the Levi-Civita connection on
(N,g)
. For
s:N\rightarrow T^{\ast}N
we take a local section such that
s(\widetilde{q})=\sum_{i}p_{i}(x)dq^{i}|_{\widetilde{q}}
. Note that
s(q)=x
and
(\nabla s)(q)=0
. Then we obtain linear maps

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{x}^{h}:&T_{q}N\longrightarrow T_{x}T^{\ast}N\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad I_{x}^{v}:T_{q}^{\ast}N\longrightarrow T_{x}T^{\ast}N\\
&\partial_{q^{i}}|_{q}\,\longmapsto\partial_{q^{i}}|_{x}\,\quad\quad\quad\raisebox{15.0pt}{ and }\quad\quad\quad\,\,dq^{i}|_{q}\,\longmapsto\partial_{p_{i}}|_{x}.\end{split}

(B.5)

Hence,
I_{x}=I_{x}^{h}\times I_{x}^{v}:T_{q}N\times T_{q}^{\ast}N\rightarrow T_{x}T^{\ast}N
is a linear symplectomorphism.

It follows that the map
I:\pi^{\ast}(TN\oplus T^{\ast}N)\longrightarrow TT^{\ast}N
is an isomorphism of symplectic vector bundles. Moreover, the pullbacks
P^{\ast}(J^{g})
and
P^{\ast}(g^{D})
induce a Hermitian structure on
TT^{\ast}N
.

Remark 446
.

Observe that
P
“splits”
X
and
R
at each point
x=(p,q)\in S^{\ast}N
. Indeed, by the formulas (
B.5
) we have

\displaystyle P_{x}^{h}(X_{x})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad P_{x}^{v}(X_{x})

\displaystyle=x,

\displaystyle P_{x}^{h}(R_{x})

\displaystyle=x^{\sharp}\quad

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad P_{x}^{v}(R_{x})

\displaystyle=0.

Definition 447
.

Let
V
be a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then the
conormal space of

V
is defined as

L^{\ast}V:=V\times(V^{\bot})^{\ast}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}\,|\,a\in V,b(v)=0\text{ for all }v\in V\}.

Remark 448
.

Observe that if we restrict
T_{x}L^{\ast}Q
to horizontal and vertical subspaces of
T_{x}T^{\ast}N
then we get

P^{h}_{x}:T_{x}^{h}L^{\ast}Q\mapsto T_{q}Q\text{ and }P^{v}_{x}:T_{x}^{v}L^{\ast}Q\mapsto L_{q}^{\ast}Q.

Indeed, since
P^{h}=d\pi
, the first mapping was already described in Remark
426
. Because
P^{h}_{x}:T_{x}^{h}L^{\ast}Q\rightarrow T_{q}Q
is an isomorphism, we get that
\dim T_{x}^{v}L^{\ast}Q=n-\dim Q=\dim L_{q}^{\ast}Q
. Since
P_{x}^{v}:T_{x}^{v}T^{\ast}N\rightarrow T^{\ast}_{q}N
is an isomorphism with an inverse
I^{v}_{x}
, it is enough to show that
I^{v}_{x}
maps every element of
L_{q}^{\ast}Q
to
T_{x}^{v}L^{\ast}Q
. But, by the definition of
I^{v}_{x}
,
I^{v}_{x}(L_{q}^{\ast}Q)
are elements of
T_{x}L^{\ast}Q
that are in
\ker d\pi
, which is
T_{x}^{v}L^{\ast}Q
.

Remark 449
.

We would like to define the Maslov index of a Hamiltonian chord
\gamma_{H}
on
L^{\ast}Q\subset T^{\ast}N
. First, we would like to trivialize the symplectic bundle
\gamma_{H}^{\ast}TT^{\ast}N
appropriately.

Let

\{\Phi_{t}:(T_{\gamma_{H}(t)}T^{\ast}N,\omega_{\gamma_{H}(t)})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})\}_{t\in[0,T]}
